That is merely a straw which shows how the wind is blowing, but if you lived on the spot you would see more what I mean. There is trouble in the air. And now that this election is coming on, I should not wonder if things came to a head. I can’t remember a single election in Wrykyn when there was not disorder in the town. And if the school is going to join in, as it probably will, I shall not be sorry when the holidays come. I know the headmaster is only waiting for an excuse to put the town out of bounds.”
“But the kids have always had a few rows on with that school in the High Street﻿—what’s its name﻿—St. Something?” said Clowes.
===Chinese===
“咱们私下说说，是的。”西摩先生承认道。
“哪一类冲突？”特雷弗问。
西摩先生说不清楚。眼下还谈不上有什么——姑且这么说——确凿的事。到目前为止也没有谁正式来告状。不过仍然——嗯，麻烦——对，麻烦。
“比方说，”他说，“我宿舍里有个孩子，林顿——你还记得他吧？——此刻正肿着嘴唇、缺了颗门牙在人群中走动。这些我当然一概不知，但我猜他是在镇上招了祸。这不过是见微知著的一根稻草，可你们若住在这儿，就更明白我的意思了。空气里有麻烦的味道。如今竞选又快到了，事情若闹到摊牌的地步，我一点也不奇怪。在雷金，我想不起哪一回竞选镇上不出乱子。要是学校也掺和进去——多半会的——那我只盼假期快点来。我知道校长正等着一个由头，把镇子划为禁区呢。”
“可是小家伙们跟大街上那个学校——叫什么来着——圣什么书院？——不是一直有小打小闹吗？”克洛斯说。

===Original===
“Jude’s,” supplied Trevor.
“St. Jude’s!” said Mr. Seymour. “Have they? I didn’t know that.”
“Oh yes. I don’t know how it started, but it’s been going on for two or three years now. It’s a School House feud really, but Dexter’s are mixed up in it somehow. If a School House fag goes downtown he runs like an antelope along the High Street, unless he’s got one or two friends with him. I saved dozens of kids from destruction when I was at school. The St. Jude’s fellows lie in wait, and dash out on them. I used to find School House fags fighting for their lives in back alleys. The enemy fled on my approach. My air of majesty overawed them.”
“But a junior school feud matters very little,” said Mr. Seymour. “You say it has been going on for three years; and I have never heard of it till now. It is when the bigger fellows get mixed up with the town that we have to interfere. I wish the headmaster would put the place out of bounds entirely until the election is over. Except at election time, the town seems to go to sleep.”
===Chinese===
“圣犹大。”特雷弗补充道。
“圣犹大！”西摩先生说，“有这事？我竟不知道。”
“可不是。怎么结的仇我不知道，反正闹了两三年了。说起来是学校宿舍（School House）的世仇，不过德克斯特宿舍不知怎么也卷了进去。学校宿舍的低年级学童若是单独下镇，跑过大街时得像羚羊一样飞奔，除非带上一两个朋友。我上学那会儿，从毁灭边缘救下的小孩数也数不清。圣犹大的家伙们埋伏着，猛地扑出来。我常在后巷里撞见学校宿舍的小家伙们正为性命苦战。敌人一见我驾到便抱头鼠窜。我那一身威严气度镇得住他们。”
“不过小孩子之间的宿怨无伤大雅，”西摩先生说，“你说它闹了三年，我至今才头回听说。要紧的是大孩子也掺和到镇上去，那我们就不得不出面了。但愿校长把那地方整个划为禁区，等竞选过去再说。除了竞选的时候，那镇子平常倒像睡着了一样。”

===Original===
“That’s what we ought to be doing,” said Clowes to Trevor. “I think we had better be off now, sir. We promised Mr. Donaldson to be in some time tonight.”
“It’s later than I thought,” said Mr. Seymour. “Good night, Clowes. How many tries was it that you scored this afternoon? Five? I wish you were still here, to score them for instead of against us. Good night, Trevor. I was glad to see they tried you for Oxford, though you didn’t get your blue. You’ll be in next year all right. Good night.”
The two Old Wrykinians walked along the road towards Donaldson’s. It was a fine night, but misty.
“Jove, I’m quite tired,” said Clowes. “Hullo!”
===Chinese===
“我们该学那镇子睡觉去了，”克洛斯对特雷弗说，“我看我们这就得告辞了，先生。我们答应过唐纳森先生今晚早些回去。”
“比我料想的晚了，”西摩先生说，“晚安，克洛斯。今天下午你达阵了几次来着？五次？你要还在这里，替我们而不是跟我们得分就好了。晚安，特雷弗。看到他们让你去试牛津的选拔，我很高兴，虽然你还没拿到蓝袍。明年准成。晚安。”
两位老雷金人沿着大路往唐纳森家走去。夜色不错，只是有雾。
“天哪，我真有点累了，”克洛斯说，“咦！”

===Original===
“What’s up?”
They were opposite Appleby’s at the moment. Clowes drew him into the shadow of the fence.
“There’s a chap breaking out. I saw him shinning down a rope. Let’s wait, and see who it is.”
A moment later somebody ran softly through the gateway and disappeared down the road that led to the town.
===Chinese===
“怎么了？”
两人此刻正走到阿普尔比宿舍对面。克洛斯把他拉进栅栏的暗影里。
“有人在翻墙夜游。我看见他顺着一根绳子溜下来的。等等看是谁。”
片刻之后，一个人轻手轻脚跑出大门，消失在通往镇子的大路尽头。

===Original===
“Who was it?” said Trevor. “I couldn’t see.”
“I spotted him all right. It was that chap who was marking me today, Stanning. Wonder what he’s after. Perhaps he’s gone to tar the statue, like O’Hara. Rather a sportsman.”
“Rather a silly idiot,” said Trevor. “I hope he gets caught.”
“You always were one of those kind sympathetic chaps,” said Clowes. “Come on, or Donaldson’ll be locking us out.”
===Chinese===
“是谁？”特雷弗说，“我没看清。”
“我看得一清二楚。就是今天盯我那家伙，斯坦宁。不知他去干什么。没准是学奥哈拉，给雕像刷柏油去了。倒真有几分胆色。”
“倒真是个蠢货。”特雷弗说，“希望他被逮住。”
“你向来就是那种心慈面软的好人，”克洛斯说，“走吧，再磨蹭唐纳森就要把我们锁在门外了。”

## XVIII Mr. Bevan Makes a Suggestion / 十八 贝文先生献策

===Original===
When one has been working hard with a single end in view, the arrival and departure of the supreme moment is apt to leave a feeling of emptiness, as if life had been drained of all its interest, and left nothing sufficiently exciting to make it worth doing. Horatius, as he followed his plough on a warm day over the corn land which his gratified country bestowed on him for his masterly handling of the traffic on the bridge, must sometimes have felt it was a little tame. The feeling is far more acute when one has been unexpectedly baulked in one’s desire for action. Sheen, for the first few days after he received Drummond’s brief note, felt that it was useless for him to try to do anything. The Fates were against him. In stories, as Mr. Anstey has pointed out, the hero is never long without his chance of retrieving his reputation. A mad bull comes into the school grounds, and he alone (the hero, not the bull) is calm. Or there is a fire, and whose is that pale and gesticulating form at the upper window? The bully’s, of course. And who is that climbing nimbly up the Virginia creeper? Why, the hero. Who else? Three hearty cheers for the plucky hero.
But in real life opportunities of distinguishing oneself are less frequent.
Sheen continued his visits to the “Blue Boar,” but more because he shrank from telling Joe Bevan that all his trouble had been for nothing, than because he had any definite object in view. It was bitter to listen to the eulogies of the pugilist, when all the while he knew that, as far as any immediate results were concerned, it did not really matter whether he boxed well or feebly. Some day, perhaps, as Mr. Bevan was fond of pointing out when he approached the subject of disadvantages of boxing, he might meet a hooligan when he was crossing a field with his sister; but he found that but small consolation. He was in the position of one who wants a small sum of ready money, and is told that, in a few years, he may come into a fortune. By the time he got a chance of proving himself a man with his hands, he would be an Old Wrykinian. He was leaving at the end of the summer term.
Jack Bruce was sympathetic, and talked more freely than was his wont.
===Chinese===
一个人朝着一个目标埋头苦干久了，那决定性时刻一旦到来又倏忽过去，心头往往空落落的，仿佛生活的兴味被抽了个干净，再也剩不下什么够刺激、值得为之活下去的东西。想当年贺拉提乌斯（Horatius）凭着他治理桥上交通的绝技叫举国感激，得赐一片谷田，此后在暖和的日子里跟在犁后走过田垄时，想必也时有那么一点乏味之感吧。而当一个人跃跃欲试却被意外地拦了道，这滋味还要刻骨得多。接到德拉蒙德那封短简之后的那几天，希恩觉得再做什么都是徒劳。命运女神铁了心跟他作对。正如安斯蒂（Anstey）先生指出的，在小说里，主人公挽回名声的机会从来不会等得太久。一头疯牛闯进校园，唯有他一人镇定自若（是主人公，不是疯牛）。或者失了火，顶层窗口那个脸色煞白、疯狂挥手的人影是谁的？当然是恶霸的。那个顺着爬山虎敏捷往上爬的又是谁？嗐，主人公呗。还能是谁？为这勇敢的主人公，来三声痛快的欢呼。
可在现实生活里，一鸣惊人的机会要稀罕得多。
希恩照旧去“蓝野猪”，但与其说是还有什么明确的目标，不如说是拉不下脸告诉乔·贝文：他这一番心血全都白费了。明知就眼下的成效而言，自己拳打得好坏其实已经无关紧要，再听这位拳师赞不绝口，实在苦涩。也许有朝一日——每当话题挨着练拳的坏处，贝文先生总爱这么指点他——他会同妹妹一道穿过田野时撞上一个流氓；可他觉出这实在算不上多大的安慰。他眼下的处境，好比一个急需一笔现钱的人，却被告知再过几年或将继承一笔家产。等到有机会用双手证明自己是条汉子的时候，他早已是雷金学院的旧生了。夏季学期一结束他就得离校。
杰克·布鲁斯很表同情，话也比平时多了。

===Original===
“I can’t understand it,” he said. “Drummond always seemed a good sort. I should have thought he would have sent you in for the house like a shot. Are you sure you put it plainly in your letter? What did you say?”
Sheen repeated the main points of his letter.
“Did you tell him who had been teaching you?”
“No. I just said I’d been boxing lately.”
“Pity,” said Jack Bruce. “If you’d mentioned that it was Joe who’d been training you, he would probably have been much more for it. You see, he couldn’t know whether you were any good or not from your letter. But if you’d told him that Joe Bevan and Hunt both thought you good, he’d have seen there was something in it.”
===Chinese===
“我真闹不明白，”他说。“德拉蒙德向来是个爽快人。我还以为他会立马把你报上去代表宿舍出战呢。你确定信里说明白了？你是怎么写的？”
希恩把信的要点复述了一遍。
“你告诉他教你是谁了吗？”
“没有。我只说我最近在练拳。”
“可惜了，”杰克·布鲁斯说。“你要是提了一句，训练你的是乔，他很可能会热心得多。你想，光凭一封信，他无从知道你到底行不行。可你要是告诉他，乔·贝文和亨特（Hunt）都说你行，他就明白这里头有真东西了。”

===Original===
“It never occurred to me. Like a fool, I was counting on the thing so much that it didn’t strike me there would be any real difficulty in getting him to see my point. Especially when he got mumps and couldn’t go in himself. Well, it can’t be helped now.”
And the conversation turned to the prospects of Jack Bruce’s father in the forthcoming election, the polling for which had just begun.
“I’m busy now,” said Bruce. “I’m not sure that I shall be able to do much sparring with you for a bit.”
“My dear chap, don’t let me﻿—”
“Oh, it’s all right, really. Taking you to the ‘Blue Boar’ doesn’t land me out of my way at all. Most of the work lies round in this direction. I call at cottages, and lug oldest inhabitants to the poll. It’s rare sport.”
===Chinese===
“我压根没想到这层。像傻子一样，我把这事指望得太足，压根没想过要让他领会我的意思会有什么真正的难处。何况他又得了腮腺炎，自己上不了场。唉，事到如今，没法子了。”
接着话题转到了杰克·布鲁斯父亲在即将到来的选举中的前景，投票刚刚开始。
“我这阵子忙，”布鲁斯说。“一时半会儿，怕是陪不了你练多少拳了。”
“亲爱的伙计，千万别为我——”
“哦，没事，真没事。送你去‘蓝野猪’一点儿也不绕路。大部分活儿都在这一片。我挨家挨户敲门，把村里最老的老人家拖去投票。可有乐子了。”

===Original===
“Does your pater know?”
“Oh, yes. He rots me about it like anything, but, all the same, I believe he’s really rather bucked because I’ve roped in quite a dozen voters who wouldn’t have stirred a yard if I hadn’t turned up. That’s where we’re scoring. Pedder hasn’t got a car yet, and these old rotters round here aren’t going to move out of their chairs to go for a ride in an ordinary cart. But they chuck away their crutches and hop into a motor like one o’clock.”
“It must be rather a rag,” said Sheen.
The car drew up at the door of the “Blue Boar.” Sheen got out and ran upstairs to the gymnasium. Joe Bevan was sparring a round with Francis. He watched them while he changed, but without the enthusiasm of which he had been conscious on previous occasions. The solid cleverness of Joe Bevan, and the quickness and cunning of the bantamweight, were as much in evidence as before, but somehow the glamour and romance which had surrounded them were gone. He no longer watched eagerly to pick up the slightest hint from these experts. He felt no more interest than he would have felt in watching a game of lawn tennis. He *had* been keen. Since his disappointment with regard to the House Boxing he had become indifferent.
Joe Bevan noticed this before he had been boxing with him a minute.
===Chinese===
“你老子知道吗？”
“哦，知道。他拿这事笑话我笑话得不行，不过话说回来，我相信他心里其实挺得意，因为我拢来了足足一打选民——要不是我上门，他们一码路也懒得挪。这正是我们得分的地方。佩德还没有汽车，这一带的老顽固们又绝不肯离开椅子去坐普通马车跑一趟。可一听说坐汽车，他们拐棍一扔，麻利地就往车里钻。”
“这差事想必挺好玩，”希恩说。
汽车在“蓝野猪”门口停住。希恩下了车，跑上楼进健身房。乔·贝文正陪弗朗西斯练一个回合。他一边换衣服一边看，却没了先前几回的那股热乎劲儿。乔·贝文那份扎实精到的功夫，那位最轻量级拳手的疾速与狡黠，都还和从前一样一望即知，可不知怎么，罩在他们周身的那圈光环和传奇色彩，消失殆尽。他不再眼巴巴地盯着这行家高手，想偷学一星半点的诀窍。他此刻的兴致，不比看一场草地网球赛更多。他原本*是*着迷过的。自打宿舍拳击赛那场失意起，他心灰了。
跟他对练还不到一分钟，乔·贝文就察觉了。

===Original===
“Hullo, sir,” he said, “what’s this? Tired today? Not feeling well? You aren’t boxing like yourself, not at all you aren’t. There’s no weight behind ’em. You’re tapping. What’s the matter with your feet, too? You aren’t getting about as quickly as I should like to see. What have you been doing to yourself?”
“Nothing that I know of,” said Sheen. “I’m sorry I’m so rotten. Let’s have another try.”
The second try proved as unsatisfactory as the first. He was listless, and his leads and counters lacked conviction.
Joe Bevan, who identified himself with his pupils with that thoroughness which is the hallmark of the first-class boxing instructor, looked so pained at his sudden loss of form, that Sheen could not resist the temptation to confide in him. After all, he must tell him some time.
“The fact is,” he said, as they sat on the balcony overlooking the river, waiting for Jack Bruce to return with his car, “I’ve had a bit of a sickener.”
===Chinese===
“哟，先生，”他说，“这是怎么了？今儿累了？身子不舒坦？您打得不像是您自个儿，一点儿都不像。拳头后面没有分量。您这是在轻叩呢。还有您的脚是怎么了？移动也不如我希望的那么利索。您把自己怎么了？”
“我自己也没觉出什么，”希恩说。“打得这么糟，抱歉。再来一回吧。”
第二回合同第一回合一样不成样子。他无精打采，出拳和还击都软弱无力。
乔·贝文把自己和学员完全融为一体——这是一流拳击教练的标志——眼看学员状态一落千丈，那副痛心疾首的模样，让希恩忍不住要向他倾吐衷肠。横竖迟早也是要告诉他的。
“实话说吧，”两人坐在俯瞰河面的阳台上，等杰克·布鲁斯开车回来，他说，“我挨了一记闷棍。”

===Original===
“I thought you’d got sick of it,” said Mr. Bevan. “Well, have a bit of a rest.”
“I don’t mean that I’m tired of boxing,” Sheen hastened to explain. “After all the trouble you’ve taken with me, it would be a bit thick if I chucked it just as I was beginning to get on. It isn’t that. But you know how keen I was on boxing for the house?”
Joe Bevan nodded.
“Did you get beat?”
“They wouldn’t let me go in,” said Sheen.
===Chinese===
“我还当您练腻了呢，”贝文先生说。“那就歇上一阵子。”
“我不是说练拳练腻了，”希恩赶紧解释。“您为我费了这么多心血，我刚好要上路就把这撂下，那也太不像话了。不是那么回事。可您知道，我原本一门心思想代表宿舍出战的？”
乔·贝文点点头。
“输了一场？”
“他们不让我上场，”希恩说。

===Original===
“But, bless me! you’d have made babies of them. What was the instructor doing? Couldn’t he see that you were good?”
“I didn’t get a chance of showing what I could do.” He explained the difficulties of the situation.
Mr. Bevan nodded his head thoughtfully.
“So naturally,” concluded Sheen, “the thing has put me out a bit. It’s beastly having nothing to work for. I’m at a loose end. Up till now, I’ve always had the thought of the House Competition to keep me going. But now﻿—well, you see how it is. It’s like running to catch a train, and then finding suddenly that you’ve got plenty of time. There doesn’t seem any point in going on running.”
“Why not Aldershot, sir?” said Mr. Bevan.
===Chinese===
“哎呀，我的天！您会把他们当娃娃耍的。那位教官干什么吃的？他就看不出您有两下子？”
“我压根没得到露一手的机会。”他把处境的难处讲了一遍。
贝文先生若有所思地点着头。
“所以自然，”希恩总结道，“这事让我有点蔫。没有了奋斗目标，这滋味真难受。我心里空落落的。到今天为止，我一直是靠着宿舍赛这个念想撑着的。可现在——唉，您也看到了。就像狂奔着去赶火车，忽然发现时间绰绰有余。再跑下去，仿佛就没了意义。”
“那为什么不去奥尔德肖特呢，先生？”贝文先生说。

===Original===
“What!” cried Sheen.
The absolute novelty of the idea, and the gorgeous possibilities of it, made him tingle from head to foot. Aldershot! Why hadn’t he thought of it before! The House Competition suddenly lost its importance in his eyes. It was a trivial affair, after all, compared with Aldershot, that Mecca of the public-school boxer.
Then the glow began to fade. Doubts crept in. He might have learned a good deal from Joe Bevan, but had he learned enough to be able to hold his own with the best boxers of all the public schools in the country? And if he had the skill to win, had he the heart? Joe Bevan had said that he would not disgrace himself again, and he felt that the chances were against his doing so, but there was the terrible possibility. He had stood up to Francis and the others, and he had taken their blows without flinching; but in these encounters there was always at the back of his mind the comforting feeling that there was a limit to the amount of punishment he would receive. If Francis happened to drive him into a corner where he could neither attack, nor defend himself against attack, he did not use his advantage to the full. He indicated rather than used it. A couple of blows, and he moved out into the open again. But in the Public Schools Competition at Aldershot there would be no quarter. There would be nothing but deadly earnest. If he allowed himself to be manoeuvred into an awkward position, only his own skill, or the call of time, could extricate him from it.
In a word, at the “Blue Boar” he sparred. At Aldershot he would have to fight. Was he capable of fighting?
Then there was another difficulty. How was he to get himself appointed as the Wrykyn lightweight representative? Now that Drummond was unable to box, Stanning would go down, as the winner of the School Competition. These things were worked by an automatic process. Sheen felt that he could beat Stanning, but he had no means of publishing this fact to the school. He could not challenge him to a trial of skill. That sort of thing was not done.
===Chinese===
“什么！”希恩失声道。
这主意的新奇，连同它那绚烂的可能，激得他从头到脚一阵酥麻。奥尔德肖特！他怎么早没想到！宿舍赛在他眼里忽然无足轻重起来。同奥尔德肖特——那座公学拳手的麦加圣城——一比，宿舍赛终究不过是桩小事。
随后那股热劲开始消退，疑虑悄悄爬了进来。他跟乔·贝文或许学到了不少，可学到的够不够他同全国公学最顶尖的拳手分庭抗礼？退一步说，就算他有夺魁的功夫，他有那份胆气吗？乔·贝文说过，他不会再丢人现眼，他自己也觉得多半不至于，可那可怕的可能是存在的。他跟弗朗西斯他们交过手，挨了拳也不曾眨眼；可在这类比试里，他心底始终揣着一点宽慰：挨的打总是有限度的。弗朗西斯若碰巧把他逼进死角，叫他攻不上去、又守不住，弗朗西斯也从不把优势用足。他只是点到为止。两拳过后，便又挪回场中。可在奥尔德肖特的公学锦标赛上，是绝没有留情这回事的。那里只有性命相搏的较真。他若容许自己被逼入窘境，能救他出来的，就只有他自己的功夫，或是裁判的一声“时间到”。
一句话，在“蓝野猪”，他练的是拳。到了奥尔德肖特，他就得真刀真枪地打。他打得起来吗？
接着还有另一重难处。他怎样才能被指派为雷金学院的轻量级代表？德拉蒙德既不能出战，按校赛冠军的身份，下去的就该是斯坦宁了。这类事走的是自动流程。希恩自觉打得过斯坦宁，可他没法把这一事实昭告全校。他总不能向斯坦宁下战书比试比试。这种事是没人干的。

===Original===
He explained this to Joe Bevan.
“Well, it’s a pity,” said Joe regretfully. “It’s a pity.”
At this moment Jack Bruce appeared.
“What’s a pity, Joe?” he asked.
“Joe wants me to go to Aldershot as a lightweight,” explained Sheen, “and I was just saying that I couldn’t, because of Stanning.”
===Chinese===
他把这层难处讲给乔·贝文听。
“唉，可惜了，”乔惋惜地说。“可惜了。”
正这时，杰克·布鲁斯来了。
“什么可惜了，乔？”他问。
“乔想让我以轻量级身份去奥尔德肖特，”希恩解释道，“我正说去不成呢，因为斯坦宁。”

===Original===
“What about Stanning?”
“He won the School Competition, you see, so they’re bound to send him down.”
“Half a minute,” said Jack Bruce. “I never thought of Aldershot for you before. It’s a jolly good idea. I believe you’d have a chance. And it’s all right about Stanning. He’s not going down. Haven’t you heard?”
“I don’t hear anything. Why isn’t he going down?”
“He’s knocked up one of his wrists. So he says.”
===Chinese===
“斯坦宁怎么了？”
“他赢了校赛，你知道，所以他们肯定派他去。”
“且慢，”杰克·布鲁斯说。“我从前竟没想到让你去奥尔德肖特。这主意妙极了。我看你有机会。至于斯坦宁，没障碍。他不去了。你没听说？”
“我什么也听不着。他为什么不去了？”
“他把一只手腕弄伤了。他自己是这么说的。”

===Original===
“How do you mean﻿—so he says?” asked Sheen.
“I believe he funks it.”
“Why? What makes you think that?”
“Oh, I don’t know. It’s only my opinion. Still, it’s a little queer. Stanning says he crocked his left wrist in the final of the House Competition.”
“Well, what’s wrong with that? Why shouldn’t he have done so?”
===Chinese===
“你这话怎么讲——他自己说的？”希恩问。
“我看他是怯场了。”
“为什么？你凭什么这么想？”
“哦，说不上来。不过是我个人的看法。可这事总有点蹊跷。斯坦宁说，他是在宿舍赛决赛里扭伤了左腕。”
“那又怎么了？他怎么就不可能扭伤？”

===Original===
Sheen objected strongly to Stanning, but he had the elements of justice in him, and he was not going to condemn him on insufficient evidence, particularly of a crime of which he himself had been guilty.
“Of course he may have done,” said Bruce. “But it’s a bit fishy that he should have been playing fives all right two days running just after the competition.”
“He might have crocked himself then.”
“Then why didn’t he say so?”
A question which Sheen found himself unable to answer.
===Chinese===
希恩对斯坦宁成见极深，但他骨子里有几分公正，不肯仅凭不充分的证据就给人定罪——尤其这桩罪，他自己也曾犯过。
“他当然可能真伤了，”布鲁斯说。“可比赛才过，他接着两天照打墙手球，打得好好的，这就有点可疑了。”
“那也可能是打墙手球时伤的。”
“那他为什么不明说？”
这个问题，希恩发现自己无言以对。

===Original===
“Then there’s nothing to prevent you fighting, sir,” said Joe Bevan, who had been listening attentively to the conversation.
“Do you really think I’ve got a chance?”
“I do, sir.”
“Of course you have,” said Jack Bruce. “You’re quite as good as Drummond was, last time I saw him box.”
“Then I’ll have a shot at it,” said Sheen.
===Chinese===
“那就没有什么拦得住您出战了，先生，”乔·贝文说，他一直在留神听这场谈话。
“您真觉得我有胜算？”
“有，先生。”
“你当然有，”杰克·布鲁斯说。“上回看德拉蒙德打拳，你已不在他之下。”
“那我就试他一试，”希恩说。

===Original===
“Good for you, sir,” cried Joe Bevan.
“Though it’ll be a bit of a job getting leave,” said Sheen. “How would you start about it, Bruce?”
“You’d better ask Spence. He’s the man to go to.”
“That’s all right. I’m rather a pal of Spence’s.”
“Ask him tonight after prep.,” suggested Bruce.
“And then you can come here regular,” said Joe Bevan, “and we’ll train you till you’re that fit you could eat bricks, and you’ll make babies of them up at Aldershot.”
===Chinese===
“好样的，先生，”乔·贝文大声叫好。
“不过请假会有点麻烦，”希恩说。“这事你会怎么着手，布鲁斯？”
“你最好去找斯彭斯。这事得找他。”
“那就成了。我跟斯彭斯还挺有交情。”
“今晚自习课后就去问他，”布鲁斯出主意。
“往后你就常来这儿，”乔·贝文说，“我们把你练到筋骨强健、吃砖头都行的地步，到时候在奥尔德肖特，管保把他们打得满地找牙。”

## VII Mr. Joe Bevan / 七 乔·贝文先生

===Original===
Almost involuntarily he staggered up to receive another blow which sent him down again.
“That’ll do,” said a voice.
Sheen got up, panting. Between him and his assailant stood a short, sturdy man in a tweed suit. He was waving Albert back, and Albert appeared to be dissatisfied. He was arguing hotly with the newcomer.
“Now, you go away,” said that worthy, mildly, “just you go away.”
Albert gave it as his opinion that the speaker would do well not to come interfering in what didn’t concern him. What he wanted, asserted Albert, was a thick ear.
“Coming pushing yourself in,” added Albert querulously.
===Chinese===
他几乎是身不由己地摇摇晃晃爬起来，又挨了一拳，再度栽倒。
“行了。”一个声音说道。
希恩爬起来，喘着粗气。他和行凶者之间站着一个矮壮结实、穿粗花呢西装的男人。他正挥手让阿尔伯特退开，而阿尔伯特一脸的不情愿，正和来人激烈地争执着。
“我说，你走吧，”这位人物温和地说，“你就走吧。”
阿尔伯特发表意见说，这位说话的最好别来多管闲事。阿尔伯特声称，他想要的是把这家伙的耳朵打厚（英俚：狠揍一顿）。
“硬要往里插一杠子。”阿尔伯特埋怨着补了一句。

===Original===
“You go away,” repeated the stranger. “You go away. I don’t want to have trouble with you.”
Albert’s reply was to hit out with his left hand in the direction of the speaker’s face. The stranger, without fuss, touched the back of Albert’s wrist gently with the palm of his right hand, and Albert, turning round in a circle, ended the manoeuvre with his back towards his opponent. He faced round again irresolutely. The thing had surprised him.
“You go away,” said the other, as if he were making the observation for the first time.
“It’s Joe Bevan,” said one of Albert’s friends, excitedly.
Albert’s jaw fell. His freckled face paled.
“You go away,” repeated the man in the tweed suit, whose conversation seemed inclined to run in a groove.
===Chinese===
“你走吧。”陌生人又说了一遍，“你走吧。我不想跟你闹不痛快。”
阿尔伯特的回答，是抡起左手朝说话人的脸上打去。那陌生人不动声色，右掌在阿尔伯特的手腕背面轻轻一搭，阿尔伯特便原地转了个圈，转完之后背朝对手。他又犹豫不决地转过身来。这一手把他惊着了。
“你走吧。”对方说，仿佛这话是头一回提起。
“他是乔·贝文。”阿尔伯特的一个朋友激动地说。
阿尔伯特下巴耷拉下来。他那张长雀斑的脸白了。
“你走吧。”穿粗花呢西装的男人又说了一遍，他的谈吐似乎就爱顺着一条辙走。

===Original===
This time Albert took the advice. His friends had already taken it.
“Thanks,” said Sheen.
“Beware,” said Mr. Bevan oracularly, “of entrance to a quarrel; but, being in, bear’t that th’ opposed may beware of thee. Always counter back when you guard. When a man shows you his right like that, always push out your hand straight. The straight left rules the boxing world. Feeling better, sir?”
“Yes, thanks.”
“He got that right in just on the spot. I was watching. When you see a man coming to hit you with his right like that, don’t you draw back. Get on top of him. He can’t hit you then.”
That feeling of utter collapse, which is the immediate result of a blow in the parts about the waistcoat, was beginning to pass away, and Sheen now felt capable of taking an interest in sublunary matters once more. His ear smarted horribly, and when he put up a hand and felt it the pain was so great that he could barely refrain from uttering a cry. But, however physically battered he might be, he was feeling happier and more satisfied with himself than he had felt for years. He had been beaten, but he had fought his best, and not given in. Some portion of his self-respect came back to him as he reviewed the late encounter.
===Chinese===
这一回阿尔伯特听从了忠告。他的朋友们早就照办了。
“多谢。”希恩说。
“争吵嘛，”贝文先生一副神谕般的口吻说，“要防患于开端；既已入局，便要顶得住，教对面的人见了你忌惮（莎士比亚语）。防守时务必随手还击。对手那样亮出右手的时候，就把你的手直直送出去。直刺左拳统治拳坛。感觉好些了吗，先生？”
“好些了，谢谢。”
“他那记右拳打得正着，我都看在眼里。见人像那样抡右拳打过来，千万别往后缩。要贴上去压住他。那样他就打不着你了。”
那种浑身瘫软的感觉——挨了一记正中马甲一带的拳之后立竿见影的后果——正开始消退，希恩觉得又能关心尘世俗事了一回。他的耳朵火辣辣地疼，抬手一摸，疼得他几乎叫出声来。可是，无论身上挨了多少下，他心里却比多年来任何时候都快活、都更看得起自己。他是被打败了，但他尽了全力，没有认输。回想方才那场遭遇战，他的自尊心回来了几分。

===Original===
Mr. Bevan regarded him approvingly.
“He was too heavy for you,” he said. “He’s a good twelve stone, I make it. I should put you at ten stone﻿—say ten stone three. Call it nine stone twelve in condition. But you’ve got pluck, sir.”
Sheen opened his eyes at this surprising statement.
“Some I’ve met would have laid down after getting that first hit, but you got up again. That’s the secret of fighting. Always keep going on. Never give in. You know what Shakespeare says about the one who first cries, ‘Hold, enough!’ Do you read Shakespeare, sir?”
“Yes,” said Sheen.
“Ah, now *he* knew his business,” said Mr. Bevan enthusiastically. “*There* was ringcraft, as you may say. *He* wasn’t a novice.”
===Chinese===
贝文先生赞许地打量着他。
“他对你来说太重了。”他说，“依我看，他足有十二英石。你嘛，我估摸十英石——就算十英石三磅。临场状态算九英石十二磅吧。不过你有胆气，先生。”
听了这番惊人的高论，希恩睁大了眼睛。
“我见过的人里，有的吃了头一拳就躺下了，可你又爬了起来。这就是打斗的秘诀：永远向前，绝不认输。头一个喊‘够了，住手！’的人会落得什么下场，莎士比亚是怎么说的，你知道吧，先生？你读莎士比亚吗？”
“读。”希恩说。
“啊，*他*可真懂行，”贝文先生热情洋溢地说，“可以说，*那*才叫台上功夫。*他*可不是生手。”

===Original===
Sheen agreed that Shakespeare had written some good things in his time.
“That’s what you want to remember. Always keep going on, as the saying is. I was fighting Dick Roberts at the National﻿—an American, he was, from San Francisco. He come at me with his right stretched out, and I think he’s going to hit me with it, when blessed if his left don’t come out instead, and, my Golly! it nearly knocked a passage through me. Just where that fellow hit you, sir, he hit me. It was just at the end of the round, and I went back to my corner. Jim Blake was seconding me. ‘What’s this, Jim?’ I says, ‘is the man mad, or what?’ ‘Why,’ he says, ‘he’s left-handed, that’s what’s the matter. Get on top of him.’ ‘Get on top of him?’ I says. ‘My Golly, I’ll get on top of the roof if he’s going to hit me another of those.’ But I kept on, and got close to him, and he couldn’t get in another of them, and he give in after the seventh round.”
“What competition was that?” asked Sheen.
Mr. Bevan laughed. “It was a twenty-round contest, sir, for seven-fifty aside and the Lightweight Championship of the World.”
Sheen looked at him in astonishment. He had always imagined professional pugilists to be bullet-headed and beetle-browed to a man. He was not prepared for one of Mr. Joe Bevan’s description. For all the marks of his profession that he bore on his face, in the shape of lumps and scars, he might have been a curate. His face looked tough, and his eyes harboured always a curiously alert, questioning expression, as if he were perpetually “sizing up” the person he was addressing. But otherwise he was like other men. He seemed also to have a pretty taste in Literature. This, combined with his strong and capable air, attracted Sheen. Usually he was shy and ill at ease with strangers. Joe Bevan he felt he had known all his life.
“Do you still fight?” he asked.
===Chinese===
希恩表示同意：莎士比亚在世时确实写过些好东西。
“这就是你要记住的。俗话说得好，永远向前。当年我在国民俱乐部（the National）跟迪克·罗伯茨（Dick Roberts）打——他是个美国人，旧金山来的。他朝我冲过来，右手伸得老长，我心想他要拿右拳打我，谁知他的左拳反倒先出来了，乖乖！那一拳差点儿在我身上打穿一条道。就是那位老兄打你的地方，先生，他打的我。那正好是回合快结束时，我回到自己角上。吉姆·布莱克（Jim Blake）给我当助手。‘这是怎么了，吉姆？’我说，‘这人疯了，还是咋的？’‘嗨，’他说，‘他是左撇子，毛病就出在这儿。贴上去压住他。’‘贴上去？’我说，‘他要再给我来那么一拳，我就贴到房顶上去。’可我坚持住了，逼到他近前，他再也腾不出手来那么一下，打到第七回合他就认输了。”
“那是什么比赛？”希恩问。
贝文先生笑了。“那是一场二十回合的赛事，先生，七百五十镑对七百五十镑的赌注，赌的是世界轻量级冠军。”
希恩惊愕地望着他。他一向想象，职业拳手无不一颗子弹头、两道凶眉。冷不丁遇上乔·贝文先生这一路的人物，他毫无思想准备。尽管脸上带着这一行留下的印记——疙瘩与伤疤——他看着简直像位副牧师。他面目坚韧，眼睛里总含着一种警觉的、打量的神气，仿佛时时刻刻在“估量”跟他说话的人。但除此之外，他与常人无异。他似乎在文学上也颇有品位。这一点，加上他那副精明干练的气派，把希恩吸引住了。他平日见了生人总是腼腆拘谨，可对乔·贝文，他却觉得像认识了一辈子。
“您还打拳吗？”他问。

===Original===
“No,” said Mr. Bevan, “I gave it up. A man finds he’s getting on, as the saying is, and it don’t do to keep at it too long. I teach and I train, but I don’t fight now.”
A sudden idea flashed across Sheen’s mind. He was still glowing with that pride which those who are accustomed to work with their brains feel when they have gone honestly through some labour of the hands. At that moment he felt himself capable of fighting the world and beating it. The small point, that Albert had knocked him out of time in less than a minute, did not damp him at all. He had started on the right road. He had done something. He had stood up to his man till he could stand no longer. An unlimited vista of action stretched before him. He had tasted the pleasure of the fight, and he wanted more.
Why, he thought, should he not avail himself of Joe Bevan’s services to help him put himself right in the eyes of the house? At the end of the term, shortly before the Public Schools’ Competitions at Aldershot, inter-house boxing cups were competed for at Wrykyn. It would be a dramatic act of reparation to the house if he could win the Lightweight cup for it. His imagination, jumping wide gaps, did not admit the possibility of his not being good enough to win it. In the scene which he conjured up in his mind he was an easy victor. After all, there was the greater part of the term to learn in, and he would have a Champion of the World to teach him.
Mr. Bevan cut in on his reflections as if he had heard them by some process of wireless telegraphy.
“Now, look here, sir,” he said, “you should let me give you a few lessons. You’re plucky, but you don’t know the game as yet. And boxing’s a thing everyone ought to know. Supposition is, you’re crossing a field or going down a street with your sweetheart or your wife”﻿—
Sheen was neither engaged nor married, but he let the point pass.
===Chinese===
“不打了，”贝文先生说，“我撂下了。俗话说，人一上年纪，自己就觉出来了，这行干得太久不行。如今我教拳、做训练指导，可不上场打了。
一个念头倏地闪过希恩的脑海。惯于脑力劳动的人，一旦老老实实干了番体力活，会泛起一股自豪的暖意——此刻他正被这股暖意烘炙着。这会儿他觉得自己能跟全世界打一架，而且能打赢。至于那个小小的事实——阿尔伯特不到一分钟就把他打得不省人事——丝毫没有扫他的兴。他已经走上正路。他干成了点什么。他挺身面对对手，一直挺到再也站不住为止。无限宽广的行动前景在他面前铺开。他尝到了打斗的乐趣，还想要更多。
何不，他想，借乔·贝文的指点，帮自己在宿舍众人眼里把名声扳回来呢？学期末，奥尔德肖特公学锦标赛前不久，雷金要举行宿舍间拳击杯赛。倘若他能为宿舍捧回轻量级奖杯，那将是对宿舍何等漂亮的补偿。他的想象力一步跨过重重沟壑，压根不容许“自己本事不够、赢不了”这种可能。在他脑中幻出的那幕场景里，他轻取胜利。毕竟，还有大半个学期可以学，而且教他的将是一位世界冠军。
贝文先生打断了他的思绪，仿佛借着某种无线电报听见了它们似的。
“我说，您听着，先生，”他说，“您该让我给您上几课。您有胆量，可您还不会这门道。况且拳击是人人该会的东西。比方说，您正穿过一片田野，或者跟心上人、跟太太在街上走”——
希恩既没订婚也没成家，但他不跟这点计较。

===Original===
—“And up comes one of these hooligans, as they call ’em. What are you going to do if he starts his games? Why, nothing, if you can’t box. You may be plucky, but you can’t beat him. And if you beat him, you’ll get half murdered yourself. What you want to do is to learn to box, and then what happens? Why, as soon as he sees you shaping, he says to himself, ‘Hullo, this chap knows too much for me. I’m off,’ and off he runs. Or supposition is, he comes for you. You don’t mind. Not you. You give him one punch in the right place, and then you go off to your tea, leaving him lying there. He won’t get up.”
“I’d like to learn,” said Sheen. “I should be awfully obliged if you’d teach me. I wonder if you could make me any good by the end of the term. The House Competitions come off then.”
“That all depends, sir. It comes easier to some than others. If you know how to shoot your left out straight, that’s as good as six months’ teaching. After that it’s all ringcraft. The straight left beats the world.”
“Where shall I find you?”
“I’m training a young chap﻿—eight stone seven, and he’s got to get down to eight stone four, for a bantamweight match﻿—at an inn up the river here. I daresay you know it, sir. Or anyone would tell you where it is. The ‘Blue Boar,’ it’s called. You come there any time you like to name, sir, and you’ll find me.”
“I should like to come every day,” said Sheen. “Would that be too often?”
===Chinese===
“——迎面来了个所谓的小流氓。他要是耍起无赖，您怎么办？嗐，您要是不会拳，就什么辙也没有。您胆子再大，也打不过他。就算打得过，自己也得落个半死。您该做的是学拳，学成了会怎样？嗐，他一瞧您摆开的架势，心里就嘀咕：‘嚯，这位爷道行太深，惹不起，我撤。’撒腿就跑。再比方说，他真冲您来了。您不慌。您才不慌呢。照准地方给他一拳，然后该喝茶喝茶去，让他躺在那儿。他起不来的。”
“我愿意学，”希恩说，“您肯教我，我感激不尽。不知学期结束前，您能不能把我调教出个样子来。宿舍赛那时候就开打。”
“那全看情形，先生。有人上手快，有人上手慢。您要是已经会把左拳直直地捅出去，那就顶得上半年的课。往后全凭台上功夫。直刺左拳打遍天下。”
“我上哪儿找您？”
“我正在给一个小伙子做降重训练——八英石七磅，得降到八英石四磅，为的是一场雏量级（bantamweight）比赛——地点就在这儿河上游的一家客栈。您多半知道那地方，先生。随便问谁都能给您指路。店名叫‘蓝野猪’。您乐意哪天来就哪天来，先生，准能在那儿找到我。”
“我想天天都来，”希恩说，“会不会太勤了？”

===Original===
“Oftener the better, sir. You can’t practise too much.”
“Then I’ll start next week. Thanks very much. By the way, I shall have to go by boat, I suppose. It isn’t far, is it? I’ve not been up the river for some time. The School generally goes downstream.”
“It’s not what you’d call far,” said Bevan. “But it would be easier for you to come by road.”
“I haven’t a bicycle.”
“Wouldn’t one of your friends lend you one?”
Sheen flushed.
===Chinese===
“越勤越好，先生。练得再多也不嫌多。”
“那我下星期就去。多谢了。对了，我想我得坐船去。不算远吧？我有些日子没往河上游去过了。学校里的人一般都往下游去。”
“算不上您说的远，”贝文说，“不过您走大路来更省事。”
“我没有自行车。”
“您的朋友里就没人借您一辆？”
希恩脸红了。

===Original===
“No, I’d better come by boat, I think. I’ll turn up on Tuesday at about five. Will that suit you?”
“Yes, sir. That will be a good time. Then I’ll say goodbye, sir, for the present.”
Sheen went back to his house in a different mood from the one in which he had left it. He did not care now when the other Seymourites looked through him.
In the passage he met Linton, and grinned pleasantly at him.
“What the dickens was that man grinning at?” said Linton to himself. “I must have a smut or something on my face.”
But a close inspection in the dormitory looking-glass revealed no blemish on his handsome features.
===Chinese===
“没有，我看我还是坐船去吧。我星期二五点钟左右来。您看合适吗？”
“合适，先生。那是个好时候。那么，先生，这厢暂且告辞了。”
希恩回宿舍时的心境，与出门时已判若两样。如今别的西摩舍友对他视而不见，他也不放在心上了。
在过道里他碰见林顿，冲他愉快地咧嘴一笑。
“那人到底冲我乐什么？”林顿暗想，“我脸上准是沾了黑灰还是怎么的。”
可他对着宿舍的镜子仔细端详，英俊的面庞上并未发现任何瑕疵。

## XIX Paving the Way / 十九 铺路

===Original===
Bruce had been perfectly correct in his suspicions. Stanning’s wrist was no more sprained than his ankle. The advisability of manufacturing an injury had come home to him very vividly on the Saturday morning following the Ripton match, when he had read the brief report of that painful episode in that week’s number of the *Field* in the school library. In the list of the Ripton team appeared the name R. Peteiro. He had heard a great deal about the dusky Riptonian when Drummond had beaten him in the Featherweights the year before. Drummond had returned from Aldershot on that occasion cheerful, but in an extremely battered condition. His appearance as he limped about the field on Sports Day had been heroic, and, in addition, a fine advertisement for the punishing powers of the Ripton champion. It is true that at least one of his injuries had been the work of a Pauline whom he had met in the opening bout; but the great majority were presents from Ripton, and Drummond had described the dusky one, in no uncertain terms, as a holy terror.
These things had sunk into Stanning’s mind. It had been generally understood at Wrykyn that Peteiro had left school at Christmas. When Stanning, through his study of the *Field*, discovered that the redoubtable boxer had been one of the team against which he had played at Ripton, and realised that, owing to Drummond’s illness, it would fall to him, if he won the House Competition, to meet this man of wrath at Aldershot, he resolved on the instant that the most persuasive of wild horses should not draw him to that military centre on the day of the Public Schools Competition. The difficulty was that he particularly wished to win the House Cup. Then it occurred to him that he could combine the two things﻿—win the competition and get injured while doing so.
Accordingly, two days after the House Boxing he was observed to issue from Appleby’s with his left arm slung in a first fifteen scarf. He was too astute to injure his right wrist. What happens to one’s left wrist at school is one’s own private business. When one injures one’s right arm, and so incapacitates oneself for form work, the authorities begin to make awkward investigations.
Mr. Spence, who looked after the school’s efforts to win medals at Aldershot, was the most disappointed person in the place. He was an enthusiastic boxer﻿—he had represented Cambridge in the Middleweights in his day﻿—and with no small trouble had succeeded in making boxing a going concern at Wrykyn. Years of failure had ended, the Easter before, in a huge triumph, when O’Hara, of Dexter’s and Drummond had won silver medals, and Moriarty, of Dexter’s, a bronze. If only somebody could win a medal this year, the tradition would be established, and would not soon die out. Unfortunately, there was not a great deal of boxing talent in the school just now. The rule that the winner at his weight in the House Competitions should represent the school at Aldershot only applied if the winner were fairly proficient. Mr. Spence exercised his discretion. It was no use sending down novices to be massacred. This year Drummond and Stanning were the only Wrykinians up to Aldershot form. Drummond would have been almost a certainty for a silver medal, and Stanning would probably have been a runner-up. And here they were, both injured; Wrykyn would not have a single representative at the Queen’s Avenue Gymnasium. It would be a setback to the cult of boxing at the school.
Mr. Spence was pondering over this unfortunate state of things when Sheen was shown in.
===Chinese===
布鲁斯的怀疑完全正确。斯坦宁的手腕也好，脚踝也好，根本没受过伤。里普顿比赛之后的那个星期六早晨，他在学校图书馆里读当周《田野》（Field）上关于那场痛心事件的简短报道，制造一处伤情的必要性便活生生地显现在他眼前了。里普顿队的名单上印着 R. 佩泰罗（R. Peteiro）的名字。去年德拉蒙德在羽量级击败那位黑黝黝的里普顿人时，关于此君的传闻他已听过不少。那一回德拉蒙德从奥尔德肖特回来时情绪高昂，身上的伤却惨不忍睹。运动日那天他一瘸一拐绕场的样子堪称悲壮，同时也就给里普顿冠军的重拳威力做了一次绝佳的广告。诚然，他的伤至少有一处是开场那一仗里一位圣保罗选手的功劳；但绝大多数都是里普顿的馈赠，而德拉蒙德也毫不含糊地把那位黑皮肤的主儿形容成一尊凶神。
这些都沉进了斯坦宁的心里。雷金学院原先普遍认为佩泰罗已在圣诞节离校。当斯坦宁通过研读《田野》发现这位可畏的拳手竟是他里普顿之行的对手之一，又意识到由于德拉蒙德患病，一旦他赢得宿舍赛，就将轮到他在奥尔德肖特去会会这位雷霆之子时，他当下拿定主意：公学锦标赛那天，就是最能说动的野马也休想把他拖到那座军营重镇去。难就难在他又特别想赢宿舍杯。随即他想到，两件事可以兼顾——赢下比赛，并在此过程中挂点彩。
于是，宿舍拳击赛两天后，有人瞧见他吊着胳膊从阿普尔比宿舍出来，左臂用一队围巾兜着。他精得很，才不会去伤右腕。在学校里，左手腕出点事纯属个人私事；若是伤了右臂、误了功课，校方就要开始尴尬的调查了。
斯彭斯先生主管学校在奥尔德肖特的夺牌大计，是全校最失望的人。他是位热心的拳击家——当年曾代表剑桥出战中量级——又费了九牛二虎之力才让拳击在雷金成了气候。连年的失败在上个复活节终于化作一场大胜：德克斯特宿舍的奥哈拉与德拉蒙德摘得银牌，同宿舍的莫里亚蒂拿了铜牌。今年只要再有人夺得奖牌，传统便算确立，一时半会儿断不了。不巧，眼下学校里的拳击人才实在寥寥。宿舍赛各级别的胜者代表学校出征奥尔德肖特——这条规定只在该胜者确有水准时才适用，由斯彭斯先生相机裁量。派生手下去任人宰割毫无意义。今年够得上奥尔德肖特水准的雷金人只有德拉蒙德和斯坦宁。德拉蒙德几乎稳拿银牌，斯坦宁大概也能得个亚军。可现在倒好，两人双双负伤；雷金在女王大道体育馆（Queen's Avenue Gymnasium）将连一名代表都没有。这将是学校拳击之风的一次挫折。
斯彭斯先生正为这不幸的局面出神，希恩被领了进来。

===Original===
“Can I speak to you for a minute, sir?” said Sheen.
“Certainly, Sheen. Take one of those cig﻿—I mean, sit down. What is it?”
Sheen had decided how to open the interview before knocking at the door. He came to the point at once.
“Do you think I could go down to Aldershot, sir?” he asked.
Mr. Spence looked surprised.
===Chinese===
“能和您说句话吗，先生？”希恩说。
“当然可以，希恩。来一支烟——我是说，坐吧。什么事？”
敲门之前，希恩就定好了开场白。他开门见山。
“您看我能去奥尔德肖特吗，先生？”他问。
斯彭斯先生面露诧异。

===Original===
“Go down? You mean﻿—? Do you want to watch the competition? Really, I don’t know if the headmaster﻿—”
“I mean, can I box?”
Mr. Spence’s look of surprise became more marked.
“Box?” he said. “But surely﻿—I didn’t know you were a boxer, Sheen.”
“I’ve only taken it up lately.”
===Chinese===
“去奥尔德肖特？你是说——？你想去看比赛？这个，我可不知道校长会不会——”
“我是说，我能上场打拳吗？”
斯彭斯先生的讶色更浓了。
“打拳？”他说，“可是——我不知道你会拳击啊，希恩。”
“我最近才练起来的。”

===Original===
“But you didn’t enter for the House Competitions, did you? What weight are you?”
“Just under ten stone.”
“A lightweight. Why, Linton boxed for your house in the Lightweights surely?”
“Yes sir. They wouldn’t let me go in.”
“You hurt yourself?”
===Chinese===
“可你没报名参加宿舍赛，对吧？你是什么体重？”
“差一点十英石。”
“轻量级。咦，你们宿舍轻量级出战的是林顿吧？”
“是的，先生。他们不让我上场。”
“你伤了？”

===Original===
“No, sir.”
“Then why wouldn’t they let you go in?”
“Drummond thought Linton was better. He didn’t know I boxed.”
“But﻿—this is very curious. I don’t understand it at all. You see, if you were not up to House form, you would hardly﻿—At Aldershot, you see, you would meet the best boxers of all the public schools.”
“Yes, sir.”
===Chinese===
“没有，先生。”
“那他们为什么不让你上场？”
“德拉蒙德认为林顿更强。他不知道我会拳击。”
“可是——这太奇怪了。我完全不明白。你知道，如果你达不到宿舍赛的水准，那很难——在奥尔德肖特，你要遇上的可是全公学最好的拳手。”
“是，先生。”

===Original===
There was a pause.
“It was like this, sir,” said Sheen nervously. “At the beginning of the term there was a bit of a row down in the town, and I got mixed up in it. And I didn’t﻿—I was afraid to join in. I funked it.”
Mr. Spence nodded. He was deeply interested now. The office of confessor is always interesting.
“Go on, Sheen. What happened then?”
“I was cut by everybody. The fellows thought I had let the house down, and it got about, and the other houses scored off them, so I had rather a rotten time.”
===Chinese===
停了片刻。
“事情是这样的，先生，”希恩紧张地说，“学期开始时，镇上出了点乱子，我被卷了进去。可我没有——我不敢上前。我怯阵了。”
斯彭斯先生点了点头。这下他来了兴致。听取忏悔的差事向来有味。
“接着说，希恩。后来呢？”
“所有人都跟我断了来往。大家觉得我丢了宿舍的脸，这事传开了，别家宿舍又拿这事踩他们，我那段日子很不好过。”

===Original===
Here it occurred to him that he was telling his story without that attention to polite phraseology which a master expects from a boy, so he amended the last sentence.
“I didn’t have a very pleasant time, sir,” was his correction.
“Well?” said Mr. Spence.
“So I was a bit sick,” continued Sheen, relapsing once more into the vernacular, “and I wanted to do something to put things right again, and I met﻿—anyhow, I took up boxing. I wanted to box for the house, if I was good enough. I practised every day, and stuck to it, and after a bit I did become pretty good.”
“Well?”
===Chinese===
说到这里他想到，自己讲述的口吻少了教师期望学生应有的那份措辞体统，便把最后一句改了改。
“我那段日子过得不太愉快，先生。”这便是他的修正。
“后来呢？”斯彭斯先生说。
“所以我心里憋着一股气，”希恩接着说，不觉又滑回了大白话，“想做点什么把事情扳回来，后来我遇见——总之，我开始学拳击了。只要够格，我就想代表宿舍出战。我天天练，咬牙坚持，过了一阵还真练得相当不错。”
“后来？”

===Original===
“Then Drummond got mumps, and I wrote to him asking if I might represent the house instead of him, and I suppose he didn’t believe I was any good. At any rate, he wouldn’t let me go in. Then Joe﻿—a man who knows something about boxing﻿—suggested I should go down to Aldershot.”
“Joe?” said Mr. Spence inquiringly.
Sheen had let the name slip out unintentionally, but it was too late now to recall it.
“Joe Bevan, sir,” he said. “He used to be champion of England, lightweight.”
“Joe Bevan!” cried Mr. Spence. “Really? Why, he trained me when I boxed for Cambridge. He’s one of the best of fellows. I’ve never seen anyone who took such trouble with his man. I wish we could get him here. So it was Joe who suggested that you should go down to Aldershot? Well, he ought to know. Did he say you would have a good chance?”
===Chinese===
“后来德拉蒙德得了腮腺炎，我写信问他，能不能由我替他代表宿舍出战，大概他不信我有两下子。反正，他不肯让我上。后来乔——一个懂拳的人——建议我去奥尔德肖特。”
“乔？”斯彭斯先生探询地问。
这个名字是希恩无意间说漏的，如今收回已迟。
“乔·贝文，先生。”他说，“他从前是英格兰轻量级冠军。”
“乔·贝文！”斯彭斯先生叫道，“真的吗？嗐，我代表剑桥打拳时就是他训练的。他是数一数二的好人。我从没见过谁对门下弟子那么尽心。真希望能把他请到学校来。这么说，建议你去奥尔德肖特的就是乔？嗯，他该有数。他说你胜算不小？”

===Original===
“Yes, sir.”
“My position is this, you see, Sheen. There is nothing I should like more than to see the school represented at Aldershot. But I cannot let anyone go down, irrespective of his abilities. Aldershot is not child’s play. And in the Lightweights you get the hardest fighting of all. It wouldn’t do for me to let you go down if you are not up to the proper form. You would be half killed.”
“I should like to have a shot, sir,” said Sheen.
“Then this year, as you probably know, Ripton are sending down Peteiro for the Lightweights. He was the fellow whom Drummond only just beat last year. And you saw the state in which Drummond came back. If Drummond could hardly hold him, what would you do?”
“I believe I could beat Drummond, sir,” said Sheen.
===Chinese===
“是的，先生。”
“我的处境是这样的，你明白，希恩。我最盼的就是学校有人出征奥尔德肖特。可我不能不管本事如何就放人去。奥尔德肖特不是儿戏，而轻量级又是打得最苦的一级。你要是水准不够，我放你去就是害你被打个半死。”
“我很想试一试，先生。”希恩说。
“还有，今年里普顿要派佩泰罗打轻量级，这你多半知道。去年德拉蒙德险险才赢的就是这位。德拉蒙德回来时那副模样你也见了。德拉蒙德都快招架不住，你拿什么对付？”
“我相信我能打赢德拉蒙德，先生。”希恩说。

===Original===
Mr. Spence’s eyes opened wider. Here were brave words. This youth evidently meant business. The thing puzzled him. On the one hand, Sheen had been cut by his house for cowardice. On the other, Joe Bevan, who of all men was best able to judge, had told him that he was good enough to box at Aldershot.
“Let me think it over, Sheen,” he said. “This is a matter which I cannot decide in a moment. I will tell you tomorrow what I think about it.”
“I hope you will let me go down, sir,” said Sheen. “It’s my one chance.”
“Yes, yes, I see that, I see that,” said Mr. Spence, “but all the same﻿—well, I will think it over.”
All the rest of that evening he pondered over the matter, deeply perplexed. It would be nothing less than cruel to let Sheen enter the ring at Aldershot if he were incompetent. Boxing in the Public Schools Boxing Competition is not a pastime for the incompetent. But he wished very much that Wrykyn should be represented, and also he sympathised with Sheen’s eagerness to wipe out the stain on his honour, and the honour of the house. But, like Drummond, he could not help harbouring a suspicion that this was a pose. He felt that Sheen was intoxicated by his imagination. Everyone likes to picture himself doing dashing things in the limelight, with an appreciative multitude to applaud. Would this mood stand the test of action?
===Chinese===
斯彭斯先生的眼睛睁得更大了。好大的口气。这少年显然是动真格的。可这事让他纳闷。一边，希恩因怯懦被宿舍冷落；另一边，天下最有发言权的乔·贝文却说他够格上奥尔德肖特的拳台。
“容我想一想，希恩，”他说，“这事我不能立刻定夺。明天我把想法告诉你。”
“希望您放我去，先生，”希恩说，“这是我唯一的机会。”
“是，是，这个我明白，我明白，”斯彭斯先生说，“不过话说回来——好吧，我考虑考虑。”
那天晚上余下的时间，他一直苦苦思索这件事，左右为难。倘若希恩不济，放他上奥尔德肖特的拳台无异于残忍。公学拳击锦标赛可不是无能者的消遣。可他又极盼雷金有人出战，也体谅希恩急于洗刷自己名誉与宿舍名誉上污点的心情。但和德拉蒙德一样，他也不由得疑心这是一种姿态。他觉得希恩是被自己的想象陶醉了。人人都喜欢想象自己在聚光灯下干出豪举，赢得满堂喝彩。可这份心气经不经得起实战的检验呢？

===Original===
Against this there was the evidence of Joe Bevan. Joe had said that Sheen was worthy to fight for his school, and Joe knew.
Mr. Spence went to bed still in a state of doubt.
Next morning he hit upon a solution of the difficulty. Wandering in the grounds before school, he came upon O’Hara, who, as has been stated before, had won the Lightweights at Aldershot in the previous year. He had come to Wrykyn for the Sports. Here was the man to help him. O’Hara should put on the gloves with Sheen and report.
“I’m in rather a difficulty, O’Hara,” he said, “and you can help me.”
“What’s that?” inquired O’Hara.
===Chinese===
与此相对的，是乔·贝文的证言。乔说过，希恩配得上为学校一战，而乔懂行。
斯彭斯先生带着满腹疑团上了床。
第二天早晨，他想出了解决难题的办法。上课前他在校园里溜达，撞见了奥哈拉——如前所述，此人去年在奥尔德肖特夺得轻量级冠军，这次是回雷金看运动会的。助手就在眼前。让奥哈拉戴上拳套同希恩过过招，再汇报一番。
“我正犯难呢，奥哈拉，”他说，“你能不能帮我一把。”
“什么难处？”奥哈拉问。

===Original===
“You know both our lightweights are on the sick list? I had just resigned myself to going down to Aldershot without anyone to box, when a boy in Seymour’s volunteered for the vacant place. I don’t know if you knew him at school? Sheen. Do you remember him?”
“Sheen?” cried O’Hara in amazement. “Not *Sheen*!”
His recollections of Sheen were not conducive to a picture of him as a public-school boxer.
“Yes. I had never heard of him as a boxer. Still, he seems very anxious to go down, and he certainly has one remarkable testimonial, and as there’s no one else﻿—”
===Chinese===
“知道吧，我们的两位轻量级都上了伤病名单。我刚认了命，打算不带一名拳手去奥尔德肖特，西摩宿舍却有个孩子自告奋勇补这个空缺。不知道你上学时认不认识他？希恩。记得吗？”
“希恩？”奥哈拉惊呼道，“不会是*那个希恩*吧！”
他记忆中的希恩，实在拼凑不出一个公学拳手的形象。
“就是他。我从没听说他会拳。不过他好像非常想去，而且手里确有一份了不得的保荐，再说也没有旁的人了——”

===Original===
“And what shall I do?” asked O’Hara.
“I want you, if you will, to give him a trial in the dinner-hour. Just see if he’s any good at all. If he isn’t, of course, don’t hit him about a great deal. But if he shows signs of being a useful man, extend him. See what he can do.”
“Very well, sir,” said O’Hara.
“And you might look in at my house at teatime, if you have nothing better to do, and tell me what you think of him.”
===Chinese===
“那我做什么？”奥哈拉问。
“想请你——要是你愿意——在午饭时间考他一试。先看看他到底行不行。要是根本不行，当然别多捶他。可他若显出是块材料的苗头，就放开手脚打，看看他的斤两。”
“好的，先生。”奥哈拉说。
“要是没别的事，喝茶时请到我家来一趟，把你的看法告诉我。”

===Original===
At five o’clock, when he entered Mr. Spence’s study, O’Hara’s face wore the awestruck look of one who had seen visions.
“Well?” said Mr. Spence. “Did you find him any good?”
“Good?” said O’Hara. “He’ll beat them all. He’s a champion. There’s no stopping him.”
“What an extraordinary thing!” said Mr. Spence.
===Chinese===
五点钟，奥哈拉走进斯彭斯先生的书房，脸上带着一副如睹神迹般的敬畏神情。
“怎么样？”斯彭斯先生说，“你觉得他行吗？”
“行？”奥哈拉说，“他会把他们全都打趴下。他是位冠军。谁也拦不住他。”
“真是不可思议！”斯彭斯先生说。

## Preface / 序言

===Original===
The time of this story is a year and a term later than that of *The Gold Bat*. The history of Wrykyn in between these two books is dealt with in a number of short stories, some of them brainy in the extreme, which have appeared in various magazines. I wanted Messrs. Black to publish these, but they were light on their feet and kept away﻿—a painful exhibition of the White Feather.
P. G. Wodehouse
===Chinese===
本书故事的发生时间，比《金球棒》（*The Gold Bat*）晚一年零一个学期。两书之间雷金学院的历史，见于若干短篇——其中几篇精妙绝伦——曾刊载于各家杂志。我原想请布莱克出版公司（Messrs. Black）把它们印行出来，无奈他们脚底抹油、躲之不及——堪称亮出白羽毛的一次惨痛表演。
P. G. 沃德豪斯

## XVII Seymour’s One Success / 十七 西摩宿舍的唯一胜利

===Original===
This polite epistle, it may be mentioned, was a revised version of the one which Drummond originally wrote in reply to Sheen’s request. His first impulse had been to answer in the four brief words, “Don’t be a fool”; for Sheen’s letter had struck him as nothing more than a contemptible piece of posing, and he had all the hatred for poses which is a characteristic of the plain and straightforward type of mind. It seemed to him that Sheen, as he expressed it to himself, was trying to “do the boy hero.” In the school library, which had been stocked during the dark ages, when that type of story was popular, there were numerous school stories in which the hero retrieved a rocky reputation by thrashing the bully, displaying in the encounter an intuitive but overwhelming skill with his fists. Drummond could not help feeling that Sheen must have been reading one of these stories. It was all very fine and noble of him to want to show that he was No Coward After All, like Leo Cholmondeley or whatever his beastly name was, in *The Lads of St. Ethelberta*’s or some such piffling book; but, thought Drummond in his cold, practical way, what about the house? If Sheen thought that Seymour’s was going to chuck away all chance of winning one of the inter-house events, simply in order to give him an opportunity of doing the Young Hero, the sooner he got rid of that sort of idea, the better. If he wanted to do the Leo Cholmondeley business, let him go and chuck a kid into the river, and jump in and save him. But he wasn’t going to have the house let in for twenty Sheens.
Such were the meditations of Drummond when the infirmary attendant brought Sheen’s letter to him; and he seized pencil and paper and wrote, “Don’t be a fool.” But pity succeeded contempt, and he tore up the writing. After all, however much he had deserved it, the man had had a bad time. It was no use jumping on him. And at one time they had been pals. Might as well do the thing politely.
All of which reflections would have been prevented had Sheen thought of mentioning the simple fact that it was Joe Bevan who had given him the lessons to which he referred in his letter. But he had decided not to do so, wishing to avoid long explanations. And there was, he felt, a chance that the letter might come into other hands than those of Drummond. So he had preserved silence on that point, thereby wrecking his entire scheme.
It struck him that he might go to Linton, explain his position, and ask him to withdraw in his favour, but there were difficulties in the way of that course. There is a great deal of red tape about the athletic arrangements of a house at a public school. When once an order has gone forth, it is difficult to get it repealed. Linton had been chosen to represent the house in the Lightweights, and he would carry out orders. Only illness would prevent him appearing in the ring.
Sheen made up his mind not to try to take his place, and went through the days a victim to gloom, which was caused by other things besides his disappointment respecting the boxing competition. The Gotford examination was over now, and he was not satisfied with his performance. Though he did not know it, his dissatisfaction was due principally to the fact that, owing to his isolation, he had been unable to compare notes after the examinations with the others. Doing an examination without comparing notes subsequently with one’s rivals, is like playing golf against a bogey. The imaginary rival against whom one pits oneself never makes a mistake. Our own “howlers” stand out in all their horrid nakedness; but we do not realise that our rivals have probably made others far worse. In this way Sheen plumbed the depths of depression. The Gotford was a purely Classical examination, with the exception of one paper, a General Knowledge paper; and it was in this that Sheen fancied he had failed so miserably. His Greek and Latin verse were always good; his prose, he felt, was not altogether beyond the pale; but in the General Knowledge paper he had come down heavily. As a matter of fact, if he had only known, the paper was an exceptionally hard one, and there was not a single candidate for the scholarship who felt satisfied with his treatment of it. It was to questions ten, eleven, and thirteen of this paper that Cardew, of the School House, who had entered for the scholarship for the sole reason that competitors got excused two clear days of ordinary schoolwork, wrote the following answer:
===Chinese===
这封客客气气的回信，不妨说明，乃是德拉蒙德针对希恩的请求最初写就的那封的修订版。他最初的冲动，是用四个短字作答：“别犯傻”；因为在他看来，希恩那封信不过是一段令人生厌的装腔作势，而他对一切装腔作势怀有的憎恶，正是那种质朴直率型头脑的标记。在他看来——用他心里的话说——希恩是在“摆少年英雄的谱”。学校图书馆藏书于黑暗年代，那类小说正当流行，馆里有的是这种校园故事：主人公在一场打斗中以天纵神拳大败恶霸，从而挽回岌岌可危的名声。德拉蒙德不由觉得，希恩准是读了其中一本。他想证明自己“毕竟不是懦夫”，要学利奥·乔姆利（Leo Cholmondeley）——管他叫什么鬼名字——《圣埃塞尔伯塔的少年们》（*The Lads of St. Ethelberta*）或别的什么无聊书里那位的样，这用心固然高尚；可是，德拉蒙德以他那冷静讲实际的头脑想道，宿舍怎么办？希恩要是以为西摩宿舍肯扔掉宿舍间比赛一项冠军的全部希望，只为给他一个扮演少年英雄的机会，那他还是趁早打消这个念头为妙。他想演利奥·乔姆利那出戏，大可以找个小孩扔进河里，再跳下去救他。可他不能让宿舍给二十个希恩坑了。
医务室杂役把希恩的信送到时，德拉蒙德心里盘算的就是这些；他抓起铅笔和纸，写下“别犯傻”。可轻蔑之后继之以怜悯，他把写好的字撕了。毕竟，那人无论多么咎由自取，这阵子也够受的了。再踩上一脚毫无意义。何况他们一度还是好朋友。此事不如办得客气些。
倘若希恩想到在信里点明一个简单的事实——信中提到的那番授课，教他的正是乔·贝文——德拉蒙德的这番思虑本可全数免去。可他打定主意不说，免得解释起来话长。而且他觉得，这封信未必就落在德拉蒙德一人手里。于是他对这一点保持沉默，从而葬送了自己的全盘计划。
他想到可以去找林顿，说明自己的处境，请他让贤退赛，可这条路障碍重重。公学里宿舍的体育事务，官样文章多得可以。命令一旦下达，便难收回。林顿既已入选代表宿舍出战轻量级，就会奉命行事。除非生病，没有什么能拦住他不上拳台。
希恩打定主意不去争这个位置，日子便在阴郁中一天天挨过去；这份阴郁，除拳击赛之失望外，还有旁的来由。戈特福德考试如今考完了，他对自己场上的发挥并不满意。虽然他自己不知道，这份不满主要缘于：由于众人对他不理不睬，他考后无从与别人对答案。考完试不跟对手对答案，就好比打高尔夫跟“柏忌”（bogey，假想对手的杆数）较量：你与之较劲的那个假想对手从不失手。自己的“大乌龙”赤裸裸、触目地摆在那里，而你想不到对手多半错得更惨。就这样，希恩跌到了沮丧的谷底。戈特福德是纯粹的古典科目考试，只除一张卷子——一张通识卷；恰恰是在这张卷上，希恩自以为败得一塌糊涂。他的希腊语、拉丁语韵文素来出色；散文他也觉得不算出格；可通识卷上他栽了大跟头。其实——他要是知道就好了——那卷子出得格外之难，报考奖学金的人里没有一个对自己卷上的处理感到满意。正是在这张卷子的第十、第十一和第十三题上，学校宿舍（School House）的卡迪尤（Cardew）写下了如下答案；他报考这份奖学金的唯一原因是，参赛者可以豁免整整两天的日常功课：

===Original===

> See *Encyclopaedia Britannica*, *Times* edition.

If they really wanted to know, he said subsequently, that was the authority to go to. He himself would probably misinform them altogether.
===Chinese===

> 见《不列颠百科全书》（*Encyclopaedia Britannica*），“泰晤士报”版。

他后来说，他们要是真想知道，那才是该去查的权威。他自己嘛，多半会把他们彻底教错。

===Original===
In addition to the Gotford and the House Boxing, the House Fives now came on, and the authorities of Seymour’s were in no small perplexity. They met together in Rigby’s study to discuss the matter. Their difficulty was this. There was only one inmate of Seymour’s who had a chance of carrying off the House Fives Cup. And that was Sheen. The house was asking itself what was to be done about it.
“You see,” said Rigby, “you can look at it in two ways, whichever you like. We ought certainly to send in our best man for the pot, whatever sort of chap he is. But then, come to think of it, Sheen can’t very well be said to belong to the house at all. When a man’s been cut dead during the whole term, he can’t be looked on as one of the house very well. See what I mean?”
“Of course he can’t,” said Mill, who was second in command at Seymour’s. Mill’s attitude towards his fellow men was one of incessant hostility. He seemed to bear a grudge against the entire race.
Rigby resumed. He was a pacific person, and hated anything resembling rows in the house. He had been sorry for Sheen, and would have been glad to give him a chance of setting himself on his legs again.
“You see,” he said, “this is what I mean. We either recognise Sheen’s existence or we don’t. Follow? We can’t get him to win this Cup for us, and then, when he has done it, go on cutting him and treating him as if he didn’t belong to the house at all. I know he let the house down awfully badly in that business, but still, if he lifts the Fives Cup, that’ll square the thing. If he does anything to give the house a leg up, he must be treated as if he’d never let it down at all.”
===Chinese===
戈特福德与宿舍拳击赛之外，如今宿舍墙手球赛（fives）也接踵而至，西摩宿舍的当权者们大为犯难。他们聚在里格比（Rigby）的书房里计议此事。难处在于：整个西摩宿舍，有希望夺下宿舍墙手球杯的只有一人。那就是希恩。宿舍正在自问：这事如何是好。
“你瞧，”里格比说，“这事随你怎么看，横竖两个看法。要夺那只奖杯，我们当然该派最能打的人上，不管他是什么货色。可再一想，实在说不上希恩还算这宿舍的人。一个人整学期被人视同不存在，就很难再把他看作宿舍的一员了。明白我的意思吧？”
“当然算不上，”米尔（Mill）说。他在西摩宿舍位居第二。米尔对同类的态度是永不停歇的敌意，仿佛跟全人类都有一笔账。
里格比接着说。他是个好性子的和事佬，最恨宿舍里出现任何类似内讧的事。他一直为希恩难过，也乐得给他一个重新立足的机会。
“你瞧，”他说，“我就是这个意思：我们要么承认希恩的存在，要么不承认。懂了吧？我们不能让他替我们捧回奖杯，等他捧回来了，又接着不理他，拿他当外人。我知道那档子事他让宿舍丢了大脸，可话说回来，他要是举起墙手球杯，那就两清了。只要他为宿舍出力添彩，就得待他像从没让宿舍丢过脸一样。”

===Original===
“Of course,” said Barry. “I vote we send him in for the Fives.”
“What rot!” said Mill. “It isn’t as if none of the rest of us played fives.”
“We aren’t as good as Sheen,” said Barry.
“I don’t care. I call it rot letting a chap like him represent the house at anything. If he were the best fives-player in the world I wouldn’t let him play for the house.”
Rigby was impressed by his vehemence. He hesitated.
===Chinese===
“当然，”巴里（Barry）说，“我投票赞成派他出战墙手球。”
“胡说八道！”米尔说，“又不是咱们其余的人全都不会打墙手球。”
“可我们都不如希恩。”巴里说。
“我不管。让这么一号人代表宿舍出场，我说这就是胡闹。就算他是天下第一的墙手球手，我也不许他替宿舍打。”
里格比被他的激烈镇住了。他犹豫起来。

===Original===
“After all, Barry,” he said, “I don’t know. Perhaps it might﻿—you see, he did﻿—well, I really think we’d better have somebody else. The house has got its knife into Sheen too much just at present to want him as a representative. There’d only be sickness, don’t you think? Who else is there?”
So it came about that Menzies was chosen to uphold the house in the Fives Courts. Sheen was not surprised. But it was not pleasant. He was certainly having bad luck in his attempts to do something for the house. Perhaps if he won the Gotford they might show a little enthusiasm. The Gotford always caused a good deal of interest in the school. It was the best thing of its kind in existence at Wrykyn, and even the most abandoned loafers liked to feel that their house had won it. It was just possible, thought Sheen, that a brilliant win might change the feelings of Seymour’s towards him. He did not care for the applause of the multitude more than a boy should, but he preferred it very decidedly to the cut direct.
Things went badly for Seymour’s. Never in the history of the house, or, at any rate, in the comparatively recent history of the house, had there been such a slump in athletic trophies. To begin with, they were soundly beaten in the semifinal for the House football cup by Allardyce’s lot. With Drummond away, there was none to mark the captain of the School team at half, and Allardyce had raced through in a manner that must have compensated him to a certain extent for the poor time he had had in first fifteen matches. The game had ended in a Seymourite defeat by nineteen points to five.
Nor had the Boxing left the house in a better position. Linton fought pluckily in the Lightweights, but went down before Stanning, after beating a representative of Templar’s. Mill did not show up well in the Heavyweights, and was defeated in his first bout. Seymour’s were reduced to telling themselves how different it all would have been if Drummond had been there.
Sheen watched the Lightweight contests, and nearly danced with irritation. He felt that he could have eaten Stanning. The man was quick with his left, but he couldn’t *box*. He hadn’t a notion of sidestepping, and the uppercut appeared to be entirely outside his range. He would like to see him tackle Francis.
===Chinese===
“说到底，巴里，”他说，“我也拿不准。也许那样会——你晓得，他终究——唉，我看我们还是换个人吧。眼下宿舍对希恩记恨太深，不会愿意让他当代表。只会招人恶心，你说是不是？还有谁？”
于是，门齐斯（Menzies）被选中去墙手球场为宿舍争光了。希恩并不意外，但心里不是滋味。他想为宿舍做点什么，运气却实在不佳。倘若他赢得戈特福德，他们或许会表现出一点热情。戈特福德素来在学校里引起相当的关注，它是雷金同类奖项中的头一份，连最不可救药的懒虫也乐意觉得自己宿舍把它捧了去。希恩想，一场漂亮的胜利，说不定能扭转西摩宿舍对他的态度。他爱不爱众人的喝彩，没有超出一个少年应有的分寸，但比起被当面视而不见，他无疑宁可要前者。
西摩宿舍时运不济。宿舍史上——至少是距今不远的宿舍史上——体育奖杯从未如此凋零。头一件，他们在宿舍足球杯半决赛里被阿拉代斯（Allardyce）那伙人痛痛快快地打败了。德拉蒙德不在，半场无人盯防校队队长，阿拉代斯长驱直入，那阵势多少也补偿了他在校一线队比赛里一直坐的冷板凳。比赛终了，西摩队以五比十九败北。
拳击赛也没让宿舍的处境好到哪里去。林顿在轻量级打得勇猛，胜了坦普尔宿舍一名代表之后，倒在斯坦宁拳下。米尔在重量级表现平平，头一场便遭淘汰。西摩宿舍只好自我安慰：若是德拉蒙德在场，这一切将会多么不同。
希恩观战轻量级比赛，气得几乎手舞足蹈。他觉得自己简直能把斯坦宁生吞了。此人左手倒是快，可他不会*拳法*。侧闪一窍不通，上钩拳看来也全然超出他的本领范围。倒真想看看他碰上弗朗西斯会怎样。

===Original===
Sheen thought bitterly of Drummond. Why on earth couldn’t he have given him a chance. It was maddening.
The Fives carried on the story. Menzies was swamped by a Day’s man. He might just as well have stayed away altogether. The star of Seymour’s was very low on the horizon.
And then the house scored its one success. The headmaster announced it in the Hall after prayers in his dry, unemotional way.
“I have received the list of marks,” he said, “from the examiners for the Gotford Scholarship.” He paused. Sheen felt a sudden calm triumph flood over him. Somehow, intuitively, he knew that he had won. He waited without excitement for the next words.
“Out of a possible thousand marks, Sheen, who wins the scholarship, obtained seven hundred and one, Stanning six hundred and four, Wilson.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
希恩越想德拉蒙德越有气。他究竟为什么就不能给个机会呢。真叫人发疯。
墙手球赛续写着同样的故事。门齐斯被戴氏宿舍（Day’s）的一个人打得落花流水。他还不如干脆不来。西摩宿舍的星宿沉到了地平线上很低很低的地方。
就在这时，宿舍赢来了唯一一场胜利。晨祷之后，校长在大礼堂里以他干巴巴、不动声色的方式宣布了此事。
“我已收到名单，”他说，“戈特福德奖学金诸位阅卷人的评分。”他顿了顿。一阵平静的胜利感蓦地漫过希恩全身。不知怎的，凭直觉，他知道自己是赢了。他不动声色地等着下文。
“满分为一千分。获奖学金的希恩得七百零一分，斯坦宁六百零四分，威尔逊（Wilson）……”

===Original===
Sheen walked out of the Hall in the unique position of a Gotford winner with only one friend to congratulate him. Jack Bruce was the one. The other six hundred and thirty-three members of the school made no demonstration.
There was a pleasant custom at Seymour’s of applauding at tea any Seymourite who had won distinction, and so shed a reflected glory on the house. The head of the house would observe, “Well played, So-and-So!” and the rest of the house would express their emotion in the way that seemed best to them, to the subsequent exultation of the local crockery merchant, who had generally to supply at least a dozen fresh cups and plates to the house after one of these occasions. When it was for getting his first eleven or first fifteen cap that the lucky man was being cheered, the total of breakages sometimes ran into the twenties.
Rigby, good, easy man, was a little doubtful as to what course to pursue in the circumstances. Should he give the signal? After all, the fellow *had* won the Gotford. It was a score for the house, and they wanted all the scores they could get in these lean years. Perhaps, then, he had better.
“Well played, Sheen,” said he.
===Chinese===
希恩走出礼堂，处境堪称一绝：得了戈特福德，前来道贺的朋友却只有一个——杰克·布鲁斯（Jack Bruce）。全校其余六百三十三人毫无表示。
西摩宿舍有个可爱的风气：凡是为本宿舍赢得荣誉的人，喝茶时众人便鼓掌喝彩，让宿舍沾一分回光。宿舍长会先来一句：“打得好，某某！”随后全宿舍的人便各按各以为最好的方式抒发激情，乐得本地瓷器商人眉开眼笑——每逢这种场合之后，他照例得给宿舍补送至少一打崭新的杯盘。若被喝彩的幸运儿是因入选板球一线或足球一线队，破损总数有时高达二十来件。
里格比是个宽厚随和的人，眼下面临这局面，一时拿不准该怎么办。要不要发这个信号呢？毕竟，这家伙*确实*赢得了戈特福德。这算宿舍的一分，而在这歉收的年头，能到手的一分他们都要。那么，也许还是发的好。
“打得好，希恩。”他说。

===Original===
There was a dead silence. A giggle from the fags’ table showed that the comedy of the situation was not lost on the young mind.
The head of the house looked troubled. This was awfully awkward.
“Well played, Sheen,” he said again.
“Don’t mention it, Rigby,” said the winner of the Gotford politely, looking up from his plate.
===Chinese===
一片死寂。低年级小听差那桌传来一声窃笑，可见年幼的心灵也品出了这场面的滑稽。
宿舍长面有难色。这可尴尬透顶了。
“打得好，希恩。”他又说了一遍。
“不值一提，里格比。”戈特福德得主彬彬有礼地说，从盘子上抬起眼来。

## II Sheen at Home / 二 希恩在家

===Original===
On the afternoon following the Oxford A match, Sheen, of Seymour’s, was sitting over the gas-stove in his study with a Thucydides. He had been staying in that day with a cold. He was always staying in. Everyone has his hobby. That was Sheen’s.
Nobody at Wrykyn, even at Seymour’s, seemed to know Sheen very well, with the exception of Drummond; and those who troubled to think about the matter at all rather wondered what Drummond saw in him. To the superficial observer the two had nothing in common. Drummond was good at games﻿—he was in the first fifteen and the second eleven, and had won the Featherweights at Aldershot﻿—and seemed to have no interests outside them. Sheen, on the other hand, played fives for the house, and that was all. He was bad at cricket, and had given up football by special arrangement with Allardyce, on the plea that he wanted all his time for work. He was in for an in-school scholarship, the Gotford. Allardyce, though professing small sympathy with such a degraded ambition, had given him a special dispensation, and since then Sheen had retired from public life even more than he had done hitherto. The examination for the Gotford was to come off towards the end of the term.
The only other Wrykinians with whom Sheen was known to be friendly were Stanning and Attell, of Appleby’s. And here those who troubled to think about it wondered still more, for Sheen, whatever his other demerits, was not of the type of Stanning and Attell. There are certain members of every public school, just as there are certain members of every college at the universities, who are “marked men.” They have never been detected in any glaring breach of the rules, and their manner towards the powers that be is, as a rule, suave, even deferential. Yet it is one of the things which everybody knows, that they are in the black books of the authorities, and that sooner or later, in the picturesque phrase of the New Yorker, they will “get it in the neck.” To this class Stanning and Attell belonged. It was plain to all that the former was the leading member of the firm. A glance at the latter was enough to show that, whatever ambitions he may have had in the direction of villainy, he had not the brains necessary for really satisfactory evildoing. As for Stanning, he pursued an even course of life, always rigidly obeying the eleventh commandment, “thou shalt not be found out.” This kept him from collisions with the authorities; while a ready tongue and an excellent knowledge of the art of boxing﻿—he was, after Drummond, the best Lightweight in the place﻿—secured him at least tolerance at the hand of the school: and, as a matter of fact, though most of those who knew him disliked him, and particularly those who, like Drummond, were what Clowes had called the Old Brigade, he had, nevertheless, a tolerably large following. A first fifteen man, even in a bad year, can generally find boys anxious to be seen about with him.
That Sheen should have been amongst these surprised one or two people, notably Mr. Seymour, who, being games’ master had come a good deal into contact with Stanning, and had not been favourably impressed. The fact was that the keynote of Sheen’s character was a fear of giving offence. Within limits this is not a reprehensible trait in a person’s character, but Sheen overdid it, and it frequently complicated his affairs. There come times when one has to choose which of two people one shall offend. By acting in one way, we offend A. By acting in the opposite way, we annoy B. Sheen had found himself faced by this problem when he began to be friendly with Drummond. Their acquaintance, begun over a game of fives, had progressed. Sheen admired Drummond, as the type of what he would have liked to have been, if he could have managed it. And Drummond felt interested in Sheen because nobody knew much about him. He was, in a way, mysterious. Also, he played the piano really well; and Drummond at that time would have courted anybody who could play for his benefit “Mumblin’ Mose,” and didn’t mind obliging with unlimited encores.
===Chinese===
牛津A队（Oxford A）比赛之后的那个下午，西摩宿舍的希恩正坐在书房的煤气炉边，捧着一卷修昔底德（Thucydides）。那天他感冒，没出门。他是老不出门的。人各有所好。希恩的好便是这个。
雷金学院里，连西摩宿舍内部似乎也没有谁跟希恩很熟，德拉蒙德除外；而那些肯费心想想这事的人，倒纳闷德拉蒙德究竟看中了他哪一点。在浮光掠影的观察者眼里，这两人毫无共通之处。德拉蒙德精于运动——他既是橄榄球一线队、又是板球二线队成员，还在奥尔德肖特拿过羽量级冠军——而且除运动之外仿佛别无爱好。希恩呢，替宿舍打打墙手球，仅此而已。他板球打得很糟，橄榄球则经与阿拉代斯（Allardyce）特殊协商后放弃了，理由是他要把全部时间用于功课。他正备考一项校内奖学金——戈特福德（Gotford）。阿拉代斯虽然声称对如此自甘堕落的抱负不甚同情，还是给了他一项特赦；自那以后，希恩从公共生活里退隐得比先前更加彻底。戈特福德奖学金考试定在学期末举行。
众所皆知，与希恩交好的雷金人，除德拉蒙德之外就只有阿普尔比（Appleby）宿舍的斯坦宁（Stanning）和阿特尔了。对此，肯费心琢磨的人就更纳闷了，因为希恩纵有千般不是，也不是斯坦宁、阿特尔那一类人。每所公学里总有那么几个成员——正如大学里每个学院总有那么几个——是“挂了号的人物”。他们从未被当场拿获过什么了不得的违规，对当权者的态度照例也是和颜悦色，甚至毕恭毕敬。可人人都心知肚明：他们早记在校方的黑名单上，迟早——借用纽约人的生动说法——要“吃不了兜着走”。斯坦宁和阿特尔便属此类。谁都看得出来，前者是这家“商号”的主事。对后者只需看上一眼便知：无论他在为非作歹方面怀有什么样的雄心，他都缺一副能把坏事干得漂亮所需的脑子。至于斯坦宁，他行世的路数平稳，恪守第十一诫——“千万不可被人发觉”。这使他免于同当局正面相撞；而一条伶俐的舌头，加一身过硬的拳脚——论轻量级，他在本地仅次于德拉蒙德——又保他在学校里至少落得个相安无事。事实上，尽管认识他的人大多不喜欢他，尤其是德拉蒙德那样的、被克洛斯称作“老近卫军”的那批人，他身边却仍有一大帮追随者。橄榄球一线队的成员，哪怕赶上个荒年，身边总不愁找不到乐于让人看见同他结伴的少年。
希恩居然也混迹于这批追随者之间，这让一两个人颇感意外，西摩（Seymour）先生尤其如此——他身为体育老师，同斯坦宁打交道打得多了，印象殊无可取之处。其实，希恩性情的基调，是一种怕得罪人的心。凡事有度，这在一个人的品性里本算不得什么可鄙的缺点，可希恩把它做得过了头，于是他的日子也就常常跟着复杂化。人生在世，总有时节得在两个人里挑一个去得罪：这样做，开罪甲；反其道而行，又惹恼乙。希恩同德拉蒙德交好起来之后，便撞上了这道难题。两人的交情始于一局墙手球，此后日渐加深。希恩钦佩德拉蒙德，把他奉为自己有心做而做不到的那种人的典范。而德拉蒙德之所以对希恩来劲，则是因为没人摸得清希恩的底细。他多少算个神秘人物。再者，他钢琴弹得确实好；而在那段日子里，谁能替德拉蒙德弹上一曲《咕哝摩斯》（Mumblin' Mose），又不嫌麻烦地无限返场，他就能跟谁套近乎。

===Original===
So the two struck up an alliance, and as Drummond hated Stanning only a shade less than Stanning hated him, Sheen was under the painful necessity of choosing between them. He chose Drummond. Whereby he undoubtedly did wisely.
Sheen sat with his Thucydides over the gas-stove, and tried to interest himself in the doings of the Athenian expedition at Syracuse. His brain felt heavy and flabby. He realised dimly that this was because he took too little exercise, and he made a resolution to diminish his hours of work per diem by one, and to devote that one to fives. He would mention it to Drummond when he came in. He would probably come in to tea. The board was spread in anticipation of a visit from him. Herbert, the boot boy, had been despatched to the town earlier in the afternoon, and had returned with certain foodstuffs which were now stacked in an appetising heap on the table.
Sheen was just making something more or less like sense out of an involved passage of Nikias’ speech, in which that eminent general himself seemed to have only a hazy idea of what he was talking about, when the door opened.
He looked up, expecting to see Drummond, but it was Stanning. He felt instantly that “warm shooting” sensation from which David Copperfield suffered in moments of embarrassment. Since the advent of Drummond he had avoided Stanning, and he could not see him without feeling uncomfortable. As they were both in the sixth form, and sat within a couple of yards of one another every day, it will be realised that he was frequently uncomfortable.
“Great Scott!” said Stanning, “swotting?”
===Chinese===
于是两人结成了同盟。而德拉蒙德恨斯坦宁，只比斯坦宁恨他轻那么一分，希恩便落到了一个痛苦的境地：非得在他们两人之间做一选择。他选了德拉蒙德。这一选，毫无疑问是明智的。
希恩捧着修昔底德坐在煤气炉边，努力让自己对雅典人远征叙拉古的种种行状提起兴致。他的脑子又沉又绵。他隐隐意识到，这是运动太少的缘故，于是下了一个决心：每天的学习时数削减一个钟头，把这一个钟头奉献给墙手球。德拉蒙德回来时他就提这事。他多半会来喝茶。茶点已经为他将临的造访铺陈停当。擦靴小厮赫伯特（Herbert）下午早些时候被打发进城，带回了若干吃食，此刻正馋人地堆在桌上。
希恩正要从尼基阿斯（Nikias）演说里一段缠夹不清的文字里理出一点勉强像样的意思——那位显赫的将军本人对他所讲的东西似乎也只有个模模糊糊的概念——门开了。
他抬起头，满心以为是德拉蒙德，来的却是斯坦宁。他顿时感到大卫·科波菲尔（David Copperfield）每逢难为情便要犯的那种“热辣辣乱窜”的滋味。自从结识德拉蒙德以来，他一直躲着斯坦宁，而一见斯坦宁他就浑身不自在。两人同在六年级，天天同处几码之内，可见他的不自在是何等家常便饭。
“我的天！”斯坦宁说。“用功呢？”

===Original===
Sheen glanced almost guiltily at his Thucydides. Still, it was something of a relief that the other had not opened the conversation with an indictment of Drummond.
“You see,” he said apologetically, “I’m in for the Gotford.”
“So am *I*. What’s the good of swotting, though? I’m not going to do a stroke.”
As Stanning was the only one of his rivals of whom he had any real fear, Sheen might have replied with justice that, if that was the case, the more he swotted the better. But he said nothing. He looked at the stove, and dog-eared the Thucydides.
“What a worm you are, always staying in!” said Stanning.
===Chinese===
希恩几乎是心虚地瞟了一眼修昔底德。不过对方一开口没有先对德拉蒙德发起控诉，总算让人松了半口气。
“您瞧，”他带着歉意说，“我在备考戈特福德。”
“*我*也是。可用功顶什么用？我是一指头也不打算动的。”
在所有竞争者当中，希恩真正忌惮的只有斯坦宁一人，因此他满可以理直气壮地回一句：要是真这样，那你越不用功越好。但他什么也没说。他望着炉火，把修昔底德的书页折了个角。
“你这家伙真是个窝囊废，成天宅在屋里！”斯坦宁说。

===Original===
“I caught a cold watching the match yesterday.”
“You’re as flabby as﻿—” Stanning looked round for a simile, “as a doughnut. Why don’t you take some exercise?”
“I’m going to play fives, I think. I do need some exercise.”
“Fives? Why don’t you play footer?”
“I haven’t time. I want to work.”
===Chinese===
“昨天看比赛，着了凉。”
“你软得跟——”斯坦宁四下里找一个比方，“跟炸面圈似的。怎么不做点运动？”
“我打算去打墙手球。确实需要点运动。”
“墙手球？怎么不打橄榄球？”
“没时间。我要用功。”

===Original===
“What rot. I’m not doing a stroke.”
Stanning seemed to derive a spiritual pride from this admission.
“Tell you what, then,” said Stanning, “I’ll play you tomorrow after school.”
Sheen looked a shade more uncomfortable, but he made an effort, and declined the invitation.
“I shall probably be playing Drummond,” he said.
===Chinese===
“扯淡。我可一指头都不动。”
斯坦宁似乎从这句自白里获得了某种精神上的骄傲。
“那这么着吧，”斯坦宁说，“明天放学后我陪你打一场。”
希恩的脸色又添了几分别扭，但还是鼓了鼓劲，谢绝了这番好意。
“我八成要陪德拉蒙德打，”他说。

===Original===
“Oh, all right,” said Stanning. “*I* don’t care. Play whom you like.”
There was a pause.
“As a matter of fact,” resumed Stanning, “what I came here for was to tell you about last night. I got out, and went to Mitchell’s. Why didn’t you come? Didn’t you get my note? I sent a kid with it.”
Mitchell was a young gentleman of rich but honest parents, who had left the school at Christmas. He was in his father’s office, and lived in his father’s house on the outskirts of the town. From time to time his father went up to London on matters connected with business, leaving him alone in the house. On these occasions Mitchell the younger would write to Stanning, with whom when at school he had been on friendly terms; and Stanning, breaking out of his house after everybody had gone to bed, would make his way to the Mitchell residence, and spend a pleasant hour or so there. Mitchell senior owned Turkish cigarettes and a billiard table. Stanning appreciated both. There was also a piano, and Stanning had brought Sheen with him one night to play it. The getting-out and the subsequent getting-in had nearly whitened Sheen’s hair, and it was only by a series of miracles that he had escaped detection. Once, he felt, was more than enough; and when a fag from Appleby’s had brought him Stanning’s note, containing an invitation to a second jaunt of the kind, he had refused to be lured into the business again.
“Yes, I got the note,” he said.
===Chinese===
“哦，行，”斯坦宁说。“*我*无所谓。爱跟谁打跟谁打。”
一阵冷场。
“实不相瞒，”斯坦宁重新开腔，“我来是为了跟你说昨儿晚上的事。我溜出去了，去了米切尔（Mitchell）家。你怎么没来？没收到我的条子？我打发一个小家伙送的。”
米切尔是位父母有钱而又清白的少年绅士，去年圣诞节离校，如今在他父亲的办事处做事，住在他父亲坐落在镇子边缘的宅子里。他父亲时不时为生意上的事上伦敦去，把宅子留给他一个人看。每逢这种时候，小米切尔便写信给斯坦宁——在校时两人交情不错；斯坦宁便等所有人就寝之后翻出宿舍，摸到米切尔府上，在那儿惬意地消磨个把钟头。老米切尔家里藏有土耳其香烟和一张台球桌。这两样斯坦宁都欣赏有加。屋里还有一架钢琴，有一晚斯坦宁便把希恩带了去弹琴。那一路的出和进，差一点没把希恩的头发吓白，全靠一连串的奇迹才免于败露。他觉得，一回就够受的了；所以当阿普尔比宿舍的一个低年级生送来斯坦宁的条子、邀他再来一回这种夜游时，他抵住了诱惑，不肯再上这贼船。
“条子收到了，”他说。

===Original===
“Then why didn’t you come? Mitchell was asking where you were.”
“It’s so beastly risky.”
“Risky! Rot.”
“We should get sacked if we were caught.”
“Well, don’t get caught, then.”
===Chinese===
“那你怎么不来？米切尔还问你去哪儿了。”
“这险冒得太大发了。”
“冒险！扯淡。”
“被逮住是要开除的。”
“那就别被逮住。”

===Original===
Sheen registered an internal vow that he would not.
“He wanted us to go again on Monday. Will you come?”
“I﻿—don’t think I will, Stanning,” said Sheen. “It isn’t worth it.”
“You mean you funk it. That’s what’s the matter with you.”
“Yes, I do,” admitted Sheen.
===Chinese===
希恩在心里暗暗立誓：绝不会。
“他想让我们星期一再去。你来不来？”
“我——我看就不去了，斯坦宁，”希恩说。“不值当的。”
“你是说你怯了。你的毛病就出在这儿。”
“对，我是怯了，”希恩承认。

===Original===
As a rule﻿—in stories﻿—the person who owns that he is afraid gets unlimited applause and adulation, and feels a glow of conscious merit. But with Sheen it was otherwise. The admission made him, if possible, more uncomfortable than he had been before.
“Mitchell will be sick,” said Stanning.
Sheen said nothing.
Stanning changed the subject.
“Well, at any rate,” he said, “give us some tea. You seem to have been victualling for a siege.”
===Chinese===
照例——在小说里——承认自己害怕的人总能赢得无限制的掌声与颂扬，心里还泛起一阵美德的光晕。希恩却不然。这一承认，使他——如果还能更糟的话——比方才更不自在了。
“米切尔要失望了，”斯坦宁说。
希恩没做声。
斯坦宁换了个话题。
“得了，无论如何，”他说，“给我倒杯茶吧。你这架势，跟囤粮备围城似的。”

===Original===
“I’m awfully sorry,” said Sheen, turning a deeper shade of red and experiencing a redoubled attack of the warm shooting, “but the fact is, I’m waiting for Drummond.”
Stanning got up, and expressed his candid opinion of Drummond in a few words.
He said more. He described Sheen, too, in unflattering terms.
“Look here,” he said, “you may think it jolly fine to drop me just because you’ve got to know Drummond a bit, but you’ll be sick enough that you’ve done it before you’ve finished.”
“It isn’t that﻿—” began Sheen.
===Chinese===
“实在抱歉，”希恩说，脸红得更深，“热辣辣乱窜”的毛病又加倍地发作起来，“可是不巧，我在等德拉蒙德。”
斯坦宁站起身，用寥寥数语直抒了他对德拉蒙德的真实看法。
他还不止说了这些。他还把希恩也描画了一通，措辞同样不中听。
“听着，”他说，“你以为攀上了德拉蒙德一点交情，把我一脚蹬了，有多风光。等事情了结之前，有你悔的。”
“不是那么回事——”希恩分辩道。

===Original===
“I don’t care what it is. You slink about trying to avoid me all day, and you won’t do a thing I ask you to do.”
“But you see﻿—”
“Oh, shut up,” said Stanning.
===Chinese===
“我管你是什么回事。你成天躲躲闪闪地避着我，我托你一点事你也不肯办。”
“可你总得——”
“哦，闭嘴吧，”斯坦宁说。

## IX Sheen Begins His Education / 九 希恩开始受教育

===Original===
The “Blue Boar” was a picturesque inn, standing on the bank of the river Severn. It was much frequented in the summer by fishermen, who spent their days in punts and their evenings in the old oak parlour, where a picture in boxing costume of Mr. Joe Bevan, whose brother was the landlord of the inn, gazed austerely down on them, as if he disapproved of the lamentable want of truth displayed by the majority of their number. Artists also congregated there to paint the ivy-covered porch. At the back of the house were bedrooms, to which the fishermen would make their way in the small hours of a summer morning, arguing to the last as they stumbled upstairs. One of these bedrooms, larger than the others, had been converted into a gymnasium for the use of mine host’s brother. Thither he brought pugilistic aspirants who wished to be trained for various contests, and it was the boast of the “Blue Boar” that it had never turned out a loser. A reputation of this kind is a valuable asset to an inn, and the boxing world thought highly of it, in spite of the fact that it was off the beaten track. Certainly the luck of the “Blue Boar” had been surprising.
Sheen pulled steadily upstream on the appointed day, and after half an hour’s work found himself opposite the little landing-stage at the foot of the inn lawn.
His journey had not been free from adventure. On his way to the town he had almost run into Mr. Templar, and but for the lucky accident of that gentleman’s short sight must have been discovered. He had reached the landing-stage in safety, but he had not felt comfortable until he was well out of sight of the town. It was fortunate for him in the present case that he was being left so severely alone by the school. It was an advantage that nobody took the least interest in his goings and comings.
Having moored his boat and proceeded to the inn, he was directed upstairs by the landlord, who was an enlarged and coloured edition of his brother. From the other side of the gymnasium door came an unceasing and mysterious shuffling sound.
He tapped at the door and went in.
He found himself in a large, airy room, lit by two windows and a broad skylight. The floor was covered with linoleum. But it was the furniture that first attracted his attention. In a farther corner of the room was a circular wooden ceiling, supported by four narrow pillars. From the centre of this hung a ball, about the size of an ordinary football. To the left, suspended from a beam, was an enormous leather bolster. On the floor, underneath a table bearing several pairs of boxing gloves, a skipping rope, and some wooden dumbbells, was something that looked like a dozen Association footballs rolled into one. All the rest of the room, a space some few yards square, was bare of furniture. In this space a small sweater-clad youth, with a head of light hair cropped very short, was darting about and ducking and hitting out with both hands at nothing, with such a serious, earnest expression on his face that Sheen could not help smiling. On a chair by one of the windows Mr. Joe Bevan was sitting, with a watch in his hand.
===Chinese===
“蓝野猪”是一家景致如画的客栈，坐落在塞文河（Severn）河畔。夏日里渔夫们常来光顾，白天待在撑篙平底船里，晚上聚在老橡木厅中——厅里挂着一幅贝文先生的拳击装束画像，此人正是店主的兄弟；他神色严峻地俯视着众人，仿佛对这群人中大多数所表现的可叹的诚实缺失颇为不满。画家们也聚到这里来画那爬满常春藤的门廊。客栈后部是几间卧室，渔夫们会在夏日凌晨摸到那里去，上楼梯时还跌跌撞撞地争个不停。其中一间比别间宽敞的卧室，已改作健身房，供店主人的兄弟使用。他把那些指望为各种赛事受训的拳坛后进带到那里，而“蓝野猪”的自夸是：它调教出的人从未输过。这样的名声是客栈的一笔宝贵资产，拳击界对此评价甚高，尽管此地离了大路。诚然，“蓝野猪”的运气一直好得惊人。
约定的那天，希恩稳稳地划桨溯流而上，半个钟头劳作之后，发现自己已到了客栈草坪脚下那座小栈桥的对面。
他这一路并非没有险情。去镇上的途中，他差点撞见坦普尔先生（Mr. Templar），多亏那位先生近视这一侥幸，才没被发现。他安然到达栈桥，可直到把镇子远远甩在视线之外，心里才踏实下来。眼下学校对他如此彻底地不闻不问，对他倒是一件幸事。没人对他的来去有半点兴趣，这成了他的一个有利条件。
系好船，走进客栈，店主指点他上楼去——这位店主，活脱是他兄弟的放大上色版。健身房门的那一边，传来一阵经久不息的、神秘的拖踏声。
他敲了敲门，走了进去。
屋子宽敞透气，两扇窗加一大片天窗采光。地面铺着油地毡。可最先攫住他注意力的是屋里的陈设。房间远端的角落里，立着一方圆形的木质顶板，由四根细柱撑着。顶板正中悬下一只球，约有普通足球大小。左边从梁上垂下来一只硕大的皮制长枕。一张桌子底下摆着几双拳击手套、一根跳绳、几只木哑铃，而地板上还有个东西，像是一打足球滚作一团。屋子其余的地方，一块几码见方的空处，再无别的陈设。就在这块空地里，一个穿运动衫的小个子少年，剃着极短的浅色头发，正蹿来蹿去，忽而低头忽而出拳，双拳齐挥打向虚空，一脸严肃认真的神情，惹得希恩忍不住想笑。乔·贝文先生坐在一扇窗边的椅子上，手里握着一块表。
